#5749. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5748. States rally behind Catholic church in first-of-its-kind Supreme Court fight over prayer shrine

First Liberty senior counsel Ryan Gardner explains to Fox News Digital why the Kentucky dispute could shape religious land-use protections nationwide.

FIRST ON FOX: A Catholic religious community's plans to build a small shrine to the Virgin Mary on its own property have triggered a years-long legal battle with two neighbors, drawing a coalition of state attorneys general into a potential landmark religious liberty showdown before the U.S. Supreme Court.

Kentucky Attorney General Russell Coleman is leading an 18-state coalition urging the justices to reverse a ruling that blocked the roughly 600-square-foot prayer grotto in Park Hills, Kentucky, despite the project initially receiving approval from local zoning officials.

The dispute pits the Missionaries of St. John the Baptist against neighboring property owners Joel and Elizabeth Frederic, who challenged the city's approval of the outdoor shrine and ultimately prevailed in Kentucky's appellate courts.

YOU SHOULDN’T NEED A PERMIT TO PRAY IN YOUR OWN HOME — THE SUPREME COURT SHOULD AGREE

St. John Church in Park Hills, Ky., is at the center of a Supreme Court case over plans to build a prayer grotto on its property. (First Liberty Institute)

"I’m proud to stand with other Attorney General colleagues from across the country in defense of these Kentuckians’ religious liberty," Coleman said in a statement provided to Fox News Digital. "This fundamental American freedom cannot be brushed away simply by a heckler’s veto, and I’m confident the justices will agree."

The coalition's intervention comes as the Supreme Court prepares to consider whether blocking a religious structure on an organization's property constitutes a "substantial burden" on religious exercise under the Religious Land Use and Institutionalized Persons Act (RLUIPA).

The case marks the first time the Supreme Court is considering the federal law's land-use protections, according to Coleman's office, raising the stakes for churches and religious organizations nationwide.

The controversy began in 2021 when the Missionaries sought permission to construct a 16-by-39-foot grotto honoring the Virgin Mary's apparition to Saint Bernadette in Lourdes, France.

SUPREME COURT SHOWDOWN LOOMS OVER ST MICHAEL STATUE BLOCKED FROM PUBLIC SAFETY BUILDING

The property near St. John Church in Park Hills, Ky., where the Missionaries of St. John the Baptist have sought to build a prayer grotto. (First Liberty Institute)

The proposed structure would include a shrine, plaza, walking path and retaining wall, offering a quiet place for prayer and meditation.

The church building has stood on the property for nearly a century, predating Park Hills' zoning ordinance adopted in 1974, according to previous Fox News Digital reporting.

The Park Hills Board of Adjustment approved the project in April 2021. But the Frederics challenged the approval in court, arguing that the proposed construction violated local zoning requirements.

A trial court initially upheld the city's decision before the Kentucky Court of Appeals reversed, finding that the zoning board exceeded its authority in granting the necessary permits.

SUPREME COURT TO HEAR CATHOLIC PARISH'S CHALLENGE AFTER COLORADO BARRED SCHOOLS FROM UNIVERSAL PRE-K PROGRAM

The Kentucky Supreme Court affirmed the rejection of the Missionaries' federal religious land-use claim in December 2025.

Coleman previously defended the religious organization's position before Kentucky's highest court and led a 20-state effort in April urging the U.S. Supreme Court to take up the dispute.

The justices agreed in October to hear the case, with oral arguments scheduled for Jan. 20, 2027.

Ryan Gardner, senior counsel at First Liberty Institute, which represents the Missionaries, previously told Fox News Digital that the stakes extend far beyond the proposed Catholic shrine.

"This is a massive case for religious liberty because it has the potential to impact every single zoning law in this country," Gardner said in an October Fox News Digital exclusive.

The Supreme Court is seen on Capitol Hill in Washington, Dec. 17, 2024. (J. Scott Applewhite, File/AP Photo)

Gardner compared the grotto's footprint to a swimming pool, underscoring the modest size of a project that has since reached the nation's highest court.

"RLUIPA is at the intersection of two very critical rights in this country: the rights of religious liberty and property rights," Gardner said.

Congress enacted RLUIPA in 2000 to protect religious institutions from certain burdens imposed through zoning and land-use regulations.

The Missionaries argue that preventing them from constructing a religious structure on their property substantially burdens their religious exercise.

The Frederics, however, have disputed that interpretation, arguing in their Supreme Court opposition brief that the religious organization failed to demonstrate why alternative locations for the grotto were unavailable.

They also challenged claims that lower courts are fundamentally divided over the legal standard for determining substantial burdens under RLUIPA.

The Supreme Court agreed to consider the substantial-burden question, rather than every issue raised in the underlying zoning dispute.

Kentucky Solicitor General Matt Kuhn, Principal Deputy Solicitor General Jack Heyburn and Deputy Solicitor General Jacob Abrahamson prepared the multistate brief, according to Coleman's office.

What began as a proposal for a quiet place to pray has become a national battle over religious liberty, property rights and how far neighbors can go in challenging religious construction on private property.

Jasmine Baehr is a breaking news writer for Fox News Digital, where she covers politics, the military, legal debates surrounding life and family policy, as well as faith and culture.

#5745. Dem lawmaker to resign after report says he made 'unwelcome' sexual advances toward staffer

Trey Gowdy addresses the persistent problem of sexual impropriety on Capitol Hill during 'Sunday Night in America.'

A West Coast Democrat announced his resignation from the House of Representatives after their bipartisan internal watchdog stated that he could have committed criminal sexual assault against congressional staffers on multiple occasions.

An explosive report by the House Ethics Committee released on Monday claimed that Rep. Jimmy Gomez, D-Calif., violated congressional rules on sexual misconduct by making "unwelcome" advances toward a staffer for the House, disregarding the power imbalance between lawmakers and aides in the process.

The report also went a step further, warning Gomez could have violated existing law.

Gomez has since announced his intent to resign on Dec. 1.

HOUSE DEMOCRAT INVESTIGATED OVER ALLEGED 'INAPPROPRIATE SEXUAL CONTACT' WITH STAFFER

Rep. Jimmy Gomez speaks during a news conference on affordability on Capitol Hill in Washington, D.C., on Dec. 18, 2025. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

The bipartisan panel found that "Representative Gomez knew or had reason to know that his contact was not welcome" when out with the staffer, called "Witness 1," at a D.C. bar.

"There is also evidence that he knew or had reason to know his repeated advance towards Witness 1 in his congressional office was inappropriate and unwelcome. Accordingly, Representative Gomez’s conduct towards Witness 1 could implicate D.C. laws related to criminal sexual assault, including misdemeanor sexual abuse," the report said.

SWALWELL ANNOUNCES RESIGNATION FROM CONGRESS AFTER SEXUAL MISCONDUCT ALLEGATIONS TORPEDOED GUBERNATORIAL BID

Gomez has not been charged with any crime and has maintained his innocence. Fox News Digital reached out to his office for comment but did not immediately receive a response.

Gomez was a close ally of disgraced former Rep. Eric Swalwell, D-Calif., who resigned after being accused of sexual assault — which he denied. Gomez is also founder of the pro-family Congressional Dads Caucus.

The bipartisan ethics panel announced in August that it was investigating Gomez over allegations including "inappropriate sexual contact with a House staffer."

Gomez told Fox News Digital at the time that he "made personal mistakes outside my marriage" but added that his extramarital relations were consensual and that he did not break any laws.

CAPITOL HILL SEX SCANDALS TRIGGER NEW PUSH TO REDRAW THE LINE FOR LAWMAKERS

But the lawyers for one of the women interviewed by the committee disputed that in comments to Fox News Digital, accusing the congressman of sexual assault.

Monday’s report shows the committee appears to support her side of the story, and that Gomez’s actions "at various junctures suggests he knew that what he was doing was wrong."

The staffer is identified as having worked for a congressional leadership office, rather than on Gomez’s own staff.

Before coming out with her accusations, the staffer testified to the committee that she did not want Gomez "to feel upset in any way," and that she was "afraid that I was going to get in trouble at work," the report said.

"Even if Representative Gomez believed that his actions were wholly consensual, his failure to appreciate the reality of this power dynamic and thus the untenable position he put a young congressional staffer in, is inconsistent with the standards required of a Member of Congress," the report said.

The investigation into Gomez began after reports surfaced that he had been seen kissing a congressional staffer at a party attended by lawmakers and House employees.

Rep. Jimmy Gomez, a Democrat from California, sits on the House steps at the U.S. Capitol in Washington, D.C., on July 3, 2025. (Kent Nishimura/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Gomez initially disclosed two relationships involving congressional staffers, describing one as a past consensual sexual relationship and the other as "one sole occasion" of "consensual kissing."

GOP LAWMAKER'S GIFTS, 'EFFUSIVE AFFECTION' TOWARD YOUNG STAFFERS DETAILED IN EXPLOSIVE ETHICS REPORT

However, investigators found credible allegations that Gomez made unwanted sexual advances more than once toward the second woman, Witness 1, which the woman claimed was "nonconsensual whatsoever."

The woman, who was in her mid-20s at the time, testified that Gomez put his hand underneath her skirt and penetrated her with his fingers without consent during a February 2024 gathering at a bar in D.C., and testified to investigators that she fled to the bathroom crying after the incident.

Gomez defended himself against the allegation, telling the committee that the woman "did not say no," and did not "push" him away, while denying penetrating her.

Investigators described a second encounter with the woman in July 2024, claiming that Gomez grabbed her as she attempted to leave his congressional office, pulled her toward him and tried to kiss her.

She described Gomez as "[p]retty forceful" and said she repeatedly tried to push him away, the report said.

Gomez denied the allegation, maintaining that he merely hugged the woman and kissed her on the cheek.

Investigators then refuted his defense after they examined text messages suggesting Gomez was concerned about being seen with the woman by members of his staff, noting that he switched their communications to Signal and enabled disappearing messages. The committee also noted that Gomez initially disclosed only consensual kissing before later acknowledging sexual contact during his testimony.

After reviewing documents and speaking with 10 witnesses, the committee concluded that Gomez violated clauses 1 and 18(b) of the House Code of Official Conduct, which prohibit unwelcome sexual advances toward House employees and require lawmakers to conduct themselves in a manner reflecting creditably on the House.

Investigators concluded that Gomez's conduct caused the woman significant emotional distress and contributed to her decision to leave congressional employment in 2025.

"The Committee reiterates its commitment to investigating allegations and providing accountability to those who face sexual misconduct from Members and their staff. Here, the House lost Witness 1 as a competent and able staffer because of Representative Gomez’s misconduct and because of a broader culture of disrespect and toxic sexual behavior toward staff that permeates life on the Hill and is long overdue for correction," the report said.

A slew of lawmakers on both sides have been accused of sexual assault or harassment over the last year, prompting a reckoning over how Congress protects staffers from predatory work environments.

#5743. Bessent gave Iran weeks before it had ‘nothing left to trade.’ New sanctions expose what survived

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent joins 'Sunday Morning Futures' to discuss the Trump administration's economic sanctions against Iran, predicting a swift collapse of the regime's economy. Bessent also addresses the Iranian death threats that forced his family to relocate to a military base and weighs in on the compliance failures at Nvidia amid its ongoing espionage probe.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent warned in September that Iran would have "nothing left to trade" within two weeks.

Nearly two months later, the Trump administration is still targeting the overseas financial networks Tehran has used to keep money moving despite sweeping U.S. sanctions in the midst of ongoing war.

On Monday, Treasury announced another round of sanctions targeting more than two dozen individuals and companies accused of helping Iran move billions of dollars through financial networks spanning Dubai, Hong Kong and Turkey.

Bessent has repeatedly warned that Iran is nearing economic collapse, describing successive crackdowns as steps toward cutting off the regime's remaining financial lifelines. Yet each new round of sanctions has exposed more of the networks Tehran has used to keep doing business abroad — even as mounting economic pressure has failed to produce a diplomatic breakthrough.

COULD IRAN’S REGIME COLLAPSE? EXPERTS ASSESS BESSENT’S BID TO SEVER FINANCIAL LIFELINES

Secretary Scott Bessent speaks to members of the media outside the White House in Washington, D.C., on Oct. 22, 2025 (Aaron Schwartz/CNP/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

While the administration says its blockade has largely stopped Iranian oil exports, Monday's sanctions detail how Tehran has relied on currency dealers, trading companies and middlemen to conduct business abroad.

Treasury identified approximately $14.7 billion in financial activity potentially linked to Iranian shadow banking between January 2025 and August 2026, underscoring the scale of the networks Washington is now targeting.

Just three days earlier, Treasury declared that Iran had exhausted its seaborne oil supply outside the U.S. blockade, saying no new oil had been loaded onto vessels since the launch of Operation Economic Outcast in August.

"The regime has exhausted its seaborne oil supply, and it may never see the revenue from the oil it has already sold. The ship is sinking," Bessent said Friday.

In a response to Fox News Digital, Treasury said Iran has not loaded a single barrel of oil onto vessels since Aug. 25, 2026, and claimed Tehran is struggling to access foreign currency and recover proceeds from previous oil sales.

The department also provided a report showing the Iranian rial has lost roughly 25% of its value since August, while inflation reached 90% in September. Treasury said it is continuing to target alternative financial channels, including cryptocurrency exchanges, as Tehran looks for ways around U.S. restrictions.

BESSENT SAYS IRAN WILL HAVE 'NOTHING LEFT TO TRADE' WITHIN TWO WEEKS AS OIL LIFELINE DRIES UP

A cargo ship sails in the Persian Gulf toward the Strait of Hormuz, April 22, 2026. (AP Photo)

But even as the administration claims success in cutting off new oil shipments, Treasury is still pursuing Iranian operatives accused of collecting billions of dollars from previous sales. Friday's sanctions targeted four individuals involved in recovering oil revenues held overseas, some of which Treasury said had been diverted by middlemen for personal gain.

Bessent's September prediction was hardly his first warning that Tehran was approaching an economic breaking point.

Months earlier, he said U.S. sanctions were "asphyxiating" Iran's economy and warned that the country could soon be forced to shut down oil wells as storage facilities filled up.

By late August, Bessent was predicting Iran's economy could collapse within "weeks or months," as the administration launched Operation Economic Outcast, a campaign aimed at cutting off the regime's remaining revenue.

The warnings continued in October, when Bessent projected Iran's economy could shrink by more than 25% over the year ending in March 2027. Earlier this month, he described the country as experiencing one of the fastest economic collapses in history.

BESSENT WARNS IRAN'S OUTSIDE BACKERS TO 'STAY AWAY' AS TRUMP SEEKS TO 'ASPHYXIATE' TEHRAN'S ECONOMY

The administration had increasingly turned to economic pressure after months of military strikes failed to force Tehran into an agreement. Washington reimposed its naval blockade in July after an interim deal collapsed, and Secretary of State Marco Rubio said in September that the price Iran would pay going forward would be primarily economic, although military action remained an option.

But neither the military campaign nor the mounting economic pressure has produced a broader agreement. Iran continues to demand sanctions relief, access to frozen assets and an end to the blockade, while Washington is pressing Tehran for concessions on its nuclear program and regional activities.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent speaks with reporters outside the White House, Wednesday, in Washington, D.C. (AP Photo/Evan Vucci)

Despite months of escalating pressure, Treasury is still identifying overseas financial networks that have allowed Iran to conduct business outside traditional banking channels.

Iran has spent decades developing ways around American financial restrictions, relying on middlemen, shell companies and foreign currency exchanges to keep trade flowing even as Washington moves to cut off its access to international markets.

Monday's announcement detailed some of those channels, including exchange houses and trading companies operating across Iran, the United Arab Emirates, Turkey and Hong Kong.

Among the newly sanctioned entities was an Iranian currency exchange center that Treasury says facilitates tens of billions of dollars in annual trade, connecting exporters holding foreign currency with importers seeking to purchase goods abroad.

#5741. Former Navy SEAL says New Jersey’s Islamophobia bill puts protected speech in the crosshairs

Bill Brown, a Parlatore Law Group partner and former Navy SEAL, tells Fox News Digital why he believes New Jersey’s Islamophobia bill could make people think twice before criticizing religious and political ideas.

A New Jersey proposal aimed at combating anti-Muslim discrimination could make Americans think twice before criticizing religious and political ideas, a lawyer and former Navy SEAL warned.

Bill Brown, a partner at Parlatore Law Group, told Fox News Digital that Assembly Bill 1146 contains language broad enough to discourage public debate, even though the legislation expressly preserves First Amendment rights.

"The mere threat of state scrutiny is a direct assault on public debate," Brown said.

TRUMP DOJ EXPANDS RELIGIOUS LIBERTY PROTECTIONS AFTER MAJOR SUPREME COURT RULINGS

A New Jersey bill would create a state definition of Islamophobia for officials to consider in certain civil and criminal matters. An unrelated photo shows Muslim men praying in Marseille, France.

The proposal would establish a state definition of Islamophobia and require public officials and law enforcement officers to consider it when determining whether alleged discriminatory acts were motivated by anti-Muslim bias.

Brown argued that requiring officials to use a definition that includes harsh criticism and other speech could make people afraid to speak openly.

CHRISTIAN GROUPS AND PASTORS SAY NEW CALIFORNIA LGBT HATE SPEECH LAW IS DISGUISED AS INDOCTRINATION

Shiite Muslims stand over a representation of an American flag during a rally to condemn Israeli strikes on Iran, in Karachi, Pakistan, Sunday, June 22, 2025. (Fareed Khan/AP Photo)

"The First Amendment guarantees that no religion or theology is immune to criticism," he said.

Democratic Assemblyman Joe Danielsen sponsors the bill, which is co-sponsored by fellow Democrats Assemblywomen Verlina Reynolds-Jackson and Mitchelle Drulis and Assemblyman Roy Freiman. The legislation describes a uniform definition as a tool to help officials identify and investigate anti-Muslim crime, harassment and discrimination."

Fox News Digital made multiple attempts to contact Danielsen and all three co-sponsors for comment but did not receive a response.

Its definition includes "hatred or severe disparagement towards Muslim persons or their culture," as well as "rhetorical, violent, or physical anti-Muslim manifestations."

Other examples include pulling hijabs from Muslim women, discriminatory treatment and blaming Muslims collectively for wrongdoing committed by an individual or group.

CONNECTICUT BARS NATIVITY DISPLAY IN STATE CAPITOL BECAUSE IT'S 'RELIGIOUS,' LAWSUIT THREAT SAYS

The bill would create no standalone criminal offense for criticizing Islam. It directs officials to consider the definition when reviewing an alleged violation of an existing policy, law or regulation prohibiting discriminatory acts.

It also explicitly says nothing in the legislation should diminish or infringe on rights protected by the First Amendment or New Jersey’s Constitution.

AMERICA'S FIRST MUSLIM SENATOR EXPOSES A POLITICAL BLIND SPOT

Asked about that safeguard, Brown maintained that the prospect of scrutiny itself could discourage people from speaking, regardless of whether a case ultimately succeeds.

He also singled out a provision in the bill’s legislative findings that lists "anti-Islamism" among terms that may describe the same characteristics as Islamophobia. Brown argued that the wording risks blurring the distinction between prejudice against Muslim people and criticism of a political movement.

JONATHAN TURLEY: FRANCE'S FREE SPEECH CRACKDOWN SHOWS WHAT HAPPENS WHEN POLITICIANS DEFINE TRUTH

Former Navy SEAL and attorney Bill Brown warns that New Jersey’s proposed Islamophobia bill could discourage Americans from criticizing religious and political ideas. (Jahi Chikwendiu/The Washington Post via Getty Images)

"Islamism is a distinct geopolitical movement," he said. "It’s not just a religious faith."

Brown described the proposal as a "backdoor blasphemy law," arguing that government should protect people’s rights while allowing their religious beliefs to face public scrutiny.

PASTOR UNLEASHES ON DEARBORN AS RELIGIOUS TENSIONS ERUPT AT CITY COUNCIL MEETING: 'ENOUGH IS ENOUGH'

The measure would require officials to consider its definition when reviewing alleged violations of existing anti-discrimination laws, while also preserving First Amendment protections. (Tasos Katopodis/Getty Images)

The legislation excludes criticism of Muslim-majority countries comparable to criticism directed at other countries. Brown argued that criticism of religious and political ideology also needs protection.

Brown said his concerns are informed in part by his military service. He recalled responding to two suicide bombings in Iraq and said he remains committed to speaking out against jihadist ideology.

He also recalled studying Arabic at Rutgers after leaving the military, where Muslim American classmates helped him when he struggled with the language.

For Brown, the broader concern is whether Americans will feel free to challenge ideas they oppose.

"True diversity is diversity of thought," he said.

#5739. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

#5729. Nextnova unveils solar cell manufacturing facility

The new state-of-the-art factory in Oklahoma City will create about 300 jobs, strengthen the domestic solar supply chain, and establish a new benchmark for transparency and quality.

This month, Nextnova celebrated the official inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City, marking an important milestone in strengthening America’s domestic solar manufacturing industry.

The facility will manufacture high-efficiency solar cells using advanced automation, Industry 4.0 technologies, and comprehensive digital traceability.

Production is scheduled to begin in March 2027 with an initial annual capacity of 2 GW, designed to expand to 5 GW as market demand grows.

The new factory is expected to create approximately 300 skilled jobs in engineering, manufacturing, quality assurance, and operations, supporting economic growth while reinforcing the U.S. clean energy supply chain.

Nextnova celebrates the inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City. Credit: Nextnova

“Today represents an exciting milestone for Nextnova and for American solar manufacturing,” said Nathan Kii Miller, Nextnova’s chief strategy officer, in a statement. “Our Oklahoma City facility combines advanced manufacturing technology, complete supply chain transparency, and rigorous quality standards to deliver premium solar cells while strengthening a resilient domestic solar industry.”

Designed for maximum quality and reliability, the facility features fully integrated manufacturing execution systems (MES), automated inspection technologies, advanced process control, and a dedicated analytical laboratory supporting continuous quality verification throughout production.

Nextnova notes that its commitment to transparent sourcing, manufacturing excellence, and scalable production provides customers with high-performance solar cells that support the continued growth of a secure and competitive U.S. solar industry.

#5720. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.

#5711. Trump orders federal contractors to check with White House before responding to Congress

President Trump speaks at a news conference in the James Brady Press Briefing Room of the White House, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, in Washington. Jacquelyn Martin/AP hide caption

NEW YORK (AP) — President Donald Trump is directing federal contractors and subcontractors to consult with his administration about inquiries they receive from Congress and to withhold any communications that the White House considers privileged, according to a new executive order.

The companies must also coordinate with the White House and U.S. agencies on "appropriate responses" to any such requests, the order issued late Friday says. If they fail to comply, the White House warns their contracts could be terminated and their future work with the federal government suspended.

The order is the Republican administration's latest effort to resist what are expected to be wide-ranging and aggressive oversight efforts from Democrats when they take over Congress in January. Democrats have made government contracts one of their top investigative targets, noting that federal money has been flowing to companies linked with Trump's family, particularly at the Pentagon.

White House officials have already begun taking steps to exert more control over how agencies and officials respond to congressional requests, anticipating there will be a flood of investigations initiated by Democrats.

Trump's counsel, Will Scharf, told federal agencies on Monday that they must seek approval from his office and the White House's Office of Legislative Affairs before agreeing to appear before Congress. Scharf also suggested in a recent letter to Rep. Robert Garcia of California, the top Democrat on the House Oversight Committee, that some congressional investigations may amount to "blatant criminality."

Garcia sent letters this week directing members of Trump's family and top administration officials to preserve documents. Scharf sought to dismiss the requests, noting in a letter to Democratic lawmakers that they are not yet in the majority and cannot compel compliance.

Responding to the new executive order, Garcia said it was part of the administration's "blatant attack on Congress' constitutional right to conduct oversight." He said that despite efforts to impede investigations, "we're just getting started."

In a fact sheet, the White House framed the new requirements as "protecting executive privileges from congressional overreach," arguing that some congressional leaders have tried to intrude on executive branch privileges by "harassing private companies that contract with the federal government."

Presidents of both parties have long used executive privilege to resist congressional inquiries, but the claims can set up messy, protracted legal fights as parties dispute whether it applies. Executive privilege allows the president to keep information from the courts, Congress and the public to protect the confidentiality of White House deliberations, but the protection is far from absolute.

The latest executive order applies to all contractors and subcontractors, a universe of tens of thousands of businesses.

#5704. Medicare looks a bit different in 2027, so don't miss open enrollment

A counselor, right, helps a client through the Medicare signup process at a resource center for older adults in Sunrise, Florida. Medicare open enrollment season started Oct. 15 and ends Dec. 7. Joe Cavaretta/South Florida Sun Sentinel/Tribune News Service via Getty Images hide caption

Annual open enrollment is underway for Medicare, the federal program that provides health insurance to roughly 70 million people. The coverage choices are complex and often consequential.

Open enrollment runs through Dec. 7. The plan you choose takes effect on Jan. 1, for the full calendar year.

Medicare provides health insurance to people 65 or older, as well as to some younger people with disabilities or certain health conditions. But it isn't one plan. It's more like a build-your-own-insurance menu.

Roughly half of beneficiaries are enrolled in traditional Medicare, which consists of Parts A and B. Administered by the federal government, the plan covers hospital stays, skilled nursing care, doctor visits, outpatient care, and preventive screenings. Enrollees can typically see any healthcare provider who accepts Medicare, and they rarely need approval for diagnostic tests, such as MRIs and CT scans. However, there's no cap on out-of-pocket expenses in traditional Medicare, and it doesn't cover most prescriptions or dental, vision, or hearing services.

Stay up to date with our Up First newsletter, sent every weekday morning.

As a result, most traditional Medicare enrollees buy extra coverage from private insurance companies under Medicare Part D, the prescription drug plan. Some also buy a Medigap policy that covers the costs traditional Medicare leaves behind, such as deductibles, copayments, and coinsurance.

This year, the other half of Medicare beneficiaries opted for privately offered, all-in-one managed-care plans known as Medicare Advantage, usually including prescription drug coverage, dental, and vision. The drawbacks are smaller networks and a greater likelihood that treatments and medications will require prior authorization. Like beneficiaries in traditional Medicare, Medicare Advantage participants also pay Part B premiums.

"People really need to do their research before they enroll in Medicare, because there are trade-offs," said David Lipschutz, a co-director at the Center for Medicare Advocacy.

Every fall, insurers that offer Part D and Advantage plans send enrollees a critical document known as an Annual Notice of Change. It's a cheat sheet outlining what will be different in the following year: premiums, deductibles, covered drugs. Consumer advocates and Medicare experts say people sometimes make the costly mistake of throwing it out or filing it away without reading it. Can't find it? Call the insurer and ask for another copy.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services projects that the average Part D premium will increase less than $1 a month for a standalone prescription drug plan, describing the market as stable. But that average doesn't tell the whole story. An analysis by KFF, the health policy research nonprofit that includes KFF Health News, found that enrollees who stay in their prescription drug plan will see a 50% increase on average, from $34 to $51. About 10.4 million people could pay higher premiums, not including higher deductibles and coinsurance costs.

About 4 million people are in Medicare Advantage plans that won't be offered in 2027. If they do nothing, they'll be automatically enrolled in traditional Medicare. Jenny Kane/AP hide caption

Some beneficiaries who didn't have to pay monthly premiums in recent years will begin paying for prescription drug Part D coverage in 2027. While that change follows the 2025 introduction of federal caps on out-of-pocket drug expenses, it comes as the Trump administration is ending temporary federal subsidies that helped keep premiums down. The cap on out-of-pocket expenses for prescription drugs in 2027 will be $2,400, a total enrollees can opt tospread out over the course of the year.

Juliette Cubanski, who directs the Program on Medicare Policy at KFF, said people who don't shop around could be leaving money on the table.

"It is worth it to avoid any unpleasant surprises in the form of either higher premiums or higher cost sharing for covered services in 2027," she said.

An estimated 4.3 million people now have to look for a new Medicare Advantage or prescription drug plan after private insurers left the market. Those who want to stay in Medicare Advantage must select new coverage, or they'll be moved to traditional Medicare automatically. For those shopping for a new plan, low monthly premiums may be appealing, but it's important to read the fine print and add up the other costs.

"There are going to be, often, increases in cost sharing and copays, increases in out-of-pocket caps, and reductions in the scope of some of the extra benefits that Medicare Advantage plans provide," Lipschutz said.

So ask: Is my prescription covered? Is my pharmacy preferred? Is my doctor in-network? What does the dental benefit actually cover?

And pay attention to the out-of-pocket maximum for the Advantage plans, Cubanski said, so you know how much you might have to shell out in 2027.

"Look at that dollar value specifically," she said.

For those moving to traditional Medicare, it won't come with a drug plan, so enrollees must sign up for a new Part D plan if they want most prescriptions covered. Enrollees who miss that step when they are first eligible may not have drug coverage for the coming year, and they may face a late-enrollment penalty on their monthly premiums for life.

When selecting a drug plan, enrollees must also tell their insurer how they want to pay, either by getting a bill in the mail or by having the cost deducted from their Social Security. If you don't pick a payment plan, you could get dropped from coverage.

If enrollees elect to leave their Medicare Advantage plans for traditional Medicare within their first year of joining Medicare, they have a guaranteed right to buy a Medigap plan, with no health screening. After that, all bets are off: Insurers in most states can turn enrollees down or charge more because of a health condition. Connecticut, Maine, Massachusetts, and New York are exceptions.

Enrollees whose Advantage plans have not been renewed — and haven't been consolidated into another plan — can switch to traditional Medicare and buy a Medigap policy without a health screening, but they need to act before March.

Medicare experts advise enrollees to write down all their prescription drugs, as well as any procedures planned in the next year, such as a joint replacement or dental work, to help them decide which plan will cover their needs. Call a favorite doctor's office to see whether they are in-network for the plans being considered. Remember that brokers might earn commissions, especially on Advantage plans, and might not represent every plan.

For unbiased help, every state has a federally funded State Health Insurance Assistance Program that offers free plan counseling specific to your situation, in person and over the phone. To get help, go to the website at shiphelp.org/open-enrollment, email info@shiphelp.org, or call the SHIP National Technical Assistance Center at 877-839-2675.

A SHIP counselor can also help enrollees apply for benefits that help pay for Medicare premiums and prescription drug costs, such as the Medicare Savings Programs and Medicare Part D Extra Help.

KFF Health News is a national newsroom that produces in-depth journalism about health issues and is one of the core operating programs at KFF — the independent source for health policy research, polling, and journalism.

NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

#5703. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.

#5680. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.

by Maria Godoy, senior science and health editor and correspondent

Clinical services supervisor Dale Weiser poses for a portrait at the Chester County Health Department in West Chester, Pa., on Oct. 28, 2026. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption

People had warned me it might be tough to get Amish people to talk to me as a journalist — and probably impossible to get their permission to record their voice. Not a great omen for compelling radio storytelling.

Yet as I headed off to Lancaster County, Pa., last month to report on the biggest and deadliest measles outbreak in the U.S. in decades, I knew I wanted to hear directly from the community hit hardest.

So I turned to some good old shoe-leather reporting. My producer and I mapped out Amish-owned businesses in and around Lancaster County and spent a day driving around from shop to shop. At every stop, we introduced ourselves and explained the focus of our story. We asked Amish people (easily identifiable by their traditional dress) about their views on the measles outbreak and vaccination. While some declined to talk, others opened up about how measles had affected their families and communities, and why they had or hadn't decided to get vaccinated. To my surprise, one woman even let me record her voice.

Outside a farm supply store in a rural town, I met an Amish woman who was visibly pregnant. Measles can have devastating consequences during pregnancy. She told me her husband and two children had gotten vaccinated to protect her and her unborn child. She declined to give me her name at first, then got in a car driven by her hired non-Amish driver. As they exited the parking lot, the driver stopped to ask us what media outlet we were with. "Oh, NPR!" the driver said when we replied. Then she turned to her Amish passenger and told her, "Don't worry, you can trust them."

Part of gaining trust is about showing up, showing respect and really listening. Word gets around that you're trustworthy. Public health officials in Pennsylvania have also been using a similar approach to win the trust of Amish people as they seek to boost vaccination rates in the current outbreak. You can read more about it here.

#5669. California Democrat Rep. Gomez resigns following ethics report on sexual misconduct allegations

U.S. Rep. Jimmy Gomez, seen here at a Congressional hearing in March, announced Monday that he would resign following a report from the House Ethics committee on allegations of sexual misconduct. Heather Diehl/Getty Images North America hide caption

Rep. Jimmy Gomez (D-Calif) says he is resigning from Congress after a bipartisan investigation released Monday found "substantial evidence" he engaged in sexual misconduct toward a House staffer.

The House Ethics Committee concluded Gomez violated House rules by "making unwelcome sexual advances and engaging in unwelcome sexual conduct towards a House staffer and disregarding the power imbalance inherent in his relationship with House staffers."

The report also says the behavior could constitute criminal sexual assault.

Gomez has served in Congress since 2017 representing parts of Los Angeles. He lost reelection this fall to fellow Democrat Angela Gonzales-Torres. California uses a system where the top two vote-getters no matter the party advance to the general election. Gomez says his resignation will be effective Dec. 1.

Lisa Banks and Sarah Nesbitt, attorneys for the unnamed House staffer, wrote in a statement that their client testified to the committee truthfully and at personal risk.

In a statement announcing his plan to resign, Gomez wrote that he cooperated with the investigation, but has strong disagreements with the report and its conclusions.

"Among other significant deficiencies and omissions, the report does not include key portions of my testimony that are essential to a full and fair understanding of what occurred," he wrote.

"I also have serious concerns about the process. In my interview, I answered the Committee's questions only to see my statements selectively quoted, disparaged, and disregarded throughout the final report, to which I was given no opportunity to respond."

The House Ethics Committee, which includes five Democrats and five Republicans, opened its investigation into Gomez in August following a press report of Gomez kissing a staffer from another Congressional office at a party. Gomez denied those allegations.

The committee asked Gomez to disclose whether he "ever engaged in a romantic or sexual relationship or made sexual contact with a congressional staffer." Gomez disclosed interactions with two women — telling the committee the encounters were all consensual. Both women spoke to the committee.

One denied "she had ever had a nonconsensual sexual encounter" with Gomez. The other, described as Witness 1 in the report, said she had more than one encounter with Gomez and she did not consider them consensual.

She told investigators that Gomez made sexual contact without her consent at a bar in February 2024 and then was forceful with her and tried to kiss her at his congressional office in July 2024.

Gomez denied the interactions were nonconsensual. The staffer, described as Witness 1 in the committee report, eventually left her job on Capitol Hill.

"Here, the House lost Witness 1 as a competent and able staffer because of Representative Gomez's misconduct and because of a broader culture of disrespect and toxic sexual behavior toward staff that permeates life on the Hill and is long overdue for correction," the committee wrote.

The report comes amid a broader conversation about how Congress deals with the behavior of its members, especially when it comes to sexual misconduct.

In April, Democrat Eric Swalwell of California resigned his House seat amid allegations of sexual assault and misconduct. He also denied the allegations. Rep. Tony Gonzales (R-Tex.) also resigned from the House after admitting to an affair with a former staff member.

Rep. Chuck Edwards (R-N.C.) dropped his reelection bid earlier this year after the House Ethics Committee recommended he face a censure vote for allegedly harassing two young female staffers. Edwards denied the allegations.

Republican Reps. Max Miller of Ohio and Cory Mills of Florida also face House Ethics investigations stemming from allegations of domestic violence. Both deny wrongdoing.

Miller lost his reelection bid this fall and Mills lost his GOP primary.

Last week, the House Ethics Committee adopted new rules with the aim of speeding up investigations into sexual misconduct and improving transparency.

#5631. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5622. DTE Energy plans up to 150 megawatts of Michigan-based solar generation

DTE Energy recently announced plans to add up to 150 megawatts of new solar energy in Michigan, a move that will create construction jobs, support local communities, and save customers money on fuel costs, the company said.

In addition, because solar generation does not require fuel, the new projects will help reduce the company’s exposure to changing fuel prices over their operating lives. The projects will also support DTE’s work to provide customers with energy that is reliable, increasingly clean, and affordable.

The company stated that these projects are an investment in Michigan and its communities. By producing more Michigan-made energy that doesn’t require the purchase  of fuel, it can help lower energy costs for customers and reduce the reliance on fuel from outside the state. At the same time, these projects will create jobs and bring new investment and tax revenue into local communities, providing more resources for schools, first responders and the services Michigan families count on every day.

DTE is seeking competitive proposals for up to 150 megawatts of new Michigan-based solar generation. The procurement supports the requirements of the settlement agreement in Michigan Public Service Commission Case No. U-21662, approved May 15, 2025.

Projects must achieve commercial operation by June 30, 2030, be located in Michigan and connect to either the MISO transmission system or DTE’s distribution system. Interested bidders can register and obtain additional information through PowerAdvocate. Proposals are due Jan. 19, 2027, and DTE expects to execute contracts in the second or third quarter of 2027.

DTE is a producer of and investor in renewable energy in Michigan, generating enough clean energy from wind and solar to power nearly one million homes. As the company works toward net zero carbon emissions, it remains committed to growing its renewable energy portfolio while maintaining a balanced mix of energy resources that supports reliability and affordability for customers. DTE’s 2026 Integrated Resource Plan builds on that commitment, calling for an additional 15 gigawatts of renewable energy by 2042.

#5620. Sierra Club Submits Comments Urging SEC to Preserve Shareholder Rights & Climate Accountability

WASHINGTON, D.C. — On Thursday, Sierra Club submitted two comment letters opposing the Securities and Exchange Commission’s (SEC) proposal to rescind Rule 14a-8, which allows shareholders to put proposals addressing climate risks and other corporate issues before fellow investors for a vote, and amend Rule 14a-4(c), which governs how companies may exercise discretionary voting authority over shareholder proposals. The Sierra Club submitted both a technical comment and a grassroots comment, reflecting concerns from over 1,100 of its members and supporters.

Together the submissions urge the SEC to withdraw its proposed changes and preserve investors’ ability to raise concerns directly with corporate management and press companies to address material risks, including climate-related risks, that can affect their long-term financial interests.

Jessye Waxman, Campaign Advisor on Sierra Club’s Sustainable Finance Campaign, released the following statement:

“Climate change and other systemic risks have real consequences for companies, investors, and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors an important way to bring those risks to management’s attention, push for meaningful action, and hold companies accountable when they fail to act. The SEC’s proposals would weaken those tools, making it harder for investors to protect their long-term financial interests and leaving corporate management with less accountability for the risks its decisions create. The SEC’s proposals would shield corporate management from meaningful shareholder scrutiny, while undermining the very investor protections the Commission is charged with upholding.”

Rule 14a-8 establishes when public companies must include qualifying shareholder proposals in the materials they send investors ahead of shareholder meetings. The SEC’s proposed repeal would leave decisions about shareholder proposals to state law and individual companies’ governing documents, eliminating a federal framework that investors have used for more than 80 years.

Sierra Club’s technical comments explain that material risks, like climate change, can both have significant financial consequences for companies and create systemic financial risks that extend across investment portfolios and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors a way to bring these concerns to company leadership and fellow shareholders, including by surfacing risks that management may overlook or have limited incentives to address. The letter emphasizes that, for decades, this process has served the broader market by helping investors collectively identify and address financially material risks.

The letter warns that replacing a consistent federal framework with varying state laws and corporate bylaws could make shareholder engagement less predictable and push disputes over proposals into costly litigation. It points to the increase in shareholder lawsuits challenging proposal exclusions during the 2026 proxy season as an indication that disputes do not disappear when the SEC steps back from administering the process; instead, they can shift to more costly and uncertain avenues.

The submission also opposes proposed amendments to Rule 14a-4(c), which would expand company management’s discretionary authority to vote shareholders’ proxies on proposals excluded from company proxy materials, unless shareholders affirmatively opt out. Sierra Club’s letter warns that these changes could allow management to vote on proposals that investors have not had an adequate opportunity to evaluate.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5618. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5616. Pacific Power Begins New Customer Protections Against Data Centers

Regulators approve new agreement to ensure data centers pay for their own energy costs

SALEM, Oregon — The Oregon Public Utility Commission has approved an agreement between advocates and Pacific Power. Now, Pacific Power customers have a strong suite of protections against data centers’ impact on Oregonians’ energy bills.

Starting Jan. 1, 2027, new Pacific Power data centers will be held responsible for their own energy costs. These new rules will only apply to future data centers and existing ones making significant investments, which regulators identified as the most pressing challenge to address. Right now, the utility has very few existing data centers on its system.

“With a long line of data centers waiting at Pacific Power’s door, I’m confident that we have the right protections at the right time,” said Bob Jenks, Executive Director of Oregon Citizens’ Utility Board. “We were able to stop more extreme impacts on power bills just in time.”

Data centers over 100 MW will also have to pay a small surcharge to fund energy efficiency upgrades for low-income households. This includes funding for deep home retrofits, lowering energy use across the grid while saving customers on monthly bills.

“Many of the households we serve are already struggling to afford their energy bills and risk having their utilities shut off. Data centers will help fund weatherization, energy efficiency upgrades, and the critical home repairs that often prevent our clients from accessing these improvements in the first place,” said Charity Fain, Executive Director of Community Energy Project. “Requiring data centers to cover the costs of the energy infrastructure they need, rather than passing those costs on to residential customers, is an important protection for low-income households, seniors, and people with disabilities.”

Under this order, if there is a power emergency, Pacific Power can prioritize critical energy use for other customers, like renters and homeowners, by curtailing service to data centers. Increased electric demand by data centers strains the grid and increases the potential for blackouts during heat waves or hard freezes.

“This is a big win that puts Oregon at the forefront of managing data center growth the right way,” said Joshua Basofin, Oregon Clean Energy Program Director at Climate Solutions. “It protects Oregonians from rising costs and strengthens grid resilience. Data centers should not harm our ability to access essential electricity to power our lives and keep us safe all year.”

Regulators also ruled that new data centers can only be connected to Pacific Power’s grid if there is enough emissions-free electricity available to serve them. Under HB 2021, Pacific Power is required by law to serve all customers with 100% emissions-free electricity by 2040. This new rule will help manage costs for other customers outside of data centers during the transition to clean energy.

“We have long known that clean energy is the most affordable form of energy,” said Ben Brint, Senior Climate Program Director at Oregon Environmental Council. “This ruling is a win for data center accountability, and for Oregonians across the state — especially frontline communities already bearing the brunt of climate impacts and rising energy costs. It will save ratepayers money in the short and long run, allocating costs appropriately while advancing Oregon’s 100% clean energy goals.”

This change also comes at a time when many Oregonians are calling for broader changes to how the state handles data centers. Impacts on data centers are poised to be an issue again at the Oregon State Legislature in 2027.

“The POWER Act demonstrated the people’s power to fight back against the rising energy rates and ecosystem harms that large data centers inflict,” said Emily Bowes, Policy Strategist & Acting Director at Sierra Club Oregon. “In 2027, alongside new and returning climate champions, we will continue to fight for a clean energy transition in Salem and statewide.”

In 2025, the Oregon Legislature passed the POWER Act to help utilities rein in data centers’ added costs to Oregonians’ energy bills. Last fall, Portland General Electric began the work to roll out this new law in practice. In January, Pacific Power started a similar process for its customers. The next step is to address the few existing data centers on Pacific Power’s system, starting as early as summer 2027. It is unclear if the final phase will include rate adjustments.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.
