#5575. Steel solar panel frames hit the mainstream

Steel is considered the backbone of the American economy, critical for both national and economic security. It’s also the backbone of solar power construction, found commonly in the racking and mounting structures tilting solar panels toward the sun. And now, after years of tests and trials, an Ohio factory is roll-forming steel for a solar product that could use a stronger backbone — the solar panel.

A factory worker places a section of steel solar panel frames on a pallet in Welser Profile’s plant in Valley City, Ohio. Credit: Nextpower

Roll-form steel company Welser Profile began manufacturing Nextpower’s NX CoreFrame steel panel frames earlier this year at its facility in Valley City, Ohio, just 30 miles outside Cleveland, and Solar Power World was invited to view production of its first major orders before adding another line fully dedicated to the solar component before the end of the year. Module frame company Origami Solar originally had the idea that swapping aluminum frames with steel would not only save the solar industry money and cut back on carbon emissions, it would also provide more physical support to bigger and heavier solar panels.

Welser, an Austrian company with origins dating back to the 1660s, is a leader in roll-form steel profiles typically only seen in aluminum extrusions and helped produce a steel frame that’s easy for panel manufacturers to assemble and contractors to handle.

“What’s special about Welser Profile is we pre-notch features in the material before it gets formed,” said Bill Johnson, North America CEO of Welser Profile. “Everything about the solar industry is about how do we make it more efficient, cheaper, faster, better? You do that by all these little details to add value farther up the stream that minimizes labor, install cost. Our hole patterns and other features allow operators to snap things together, not bolt or weld things. Everyone is looking at how to make this work in a Lego setup, not a high-intense labor workforce installing all these panels.”

Welser is helping bring the Origami Solar idea to life, but the new owner of the tech, Nextpower, is delivering steel frames to the masses. Nextpower, a steel expert through its experience in producing tracking and mounting systems, acquired Origami in 2025 and will use its existing network of steel partners to convince more PV project developers to consider an alternative to aluminum.

“Nextpower has launched over 30 manufacturing facilities in the U.S. since 2020. Nextpower buys over 700,000 short tons of primary U.S. steel annually, which is more than a small car company,” said Jenya Meydbray, VP and general manager of Nextpower’s steel frames business. “Nextpower has had [the steel frame] for one year, building on the years of Origami’s engineering with Welser’s support. What we’re doing today is launching into high-scale, high-volume production here in Ohio.”

The solar industry defaulted to aluminum panel frames because the material is lightweight, easy to handle and less susceptible to corrosion. But as the solar industry matured, more environmental and practical issues became associated with the metal. Aluminum production on average has a five-times larger carbon footprint than steel production, a difficult point to ignore in the inherently green solar industry. Solar panels have also gotten larger in surface area and use less glass to cut weight. Aluminum might no longer be the ideal material to physically support these “big, floppy modules.”

A steel coil feeds into a roll-forming machine producing solar panel frames. Credit: Nextpower

“Steel provides a far better structural support for the [panel] than aluminum does. That was the initial observation that the Origami founders made,” Meydbray said. “They landed on this specific [roll-form] profile. Roll-forming is a precision process. We’re making millions of sticks of solar panel frames a month, and all of them have to be within sub-millimeter of precision across the full length. Welser is 100 years older than America, so Welser is definitely the most experienced in this art form of steel roll-forming.”

Welser set up North American operations in 2018 after acquiring an automotive roll-form operation in Valley City. The company established a second location in the same industrial park to accommodate its growing client base in the solar, storage and energy industries.

Welser was one of the first roll-form partners to validate Magnelis — a zinc magnesium coating that has “self-healing” properties is capable of lasting 25 years in the field — and found it a great steel coating for solar energy applications. It’s one of the features of NX CoreFrame, Nextpower’s steel solar module frame.

“Coming up with a new coating on steel, you don’t just throw it on the steel and sell it to the market. You have to trial it, validate it, make sure it works,” Johnson said. “Raw zinc — the coating has to be too thick for the longevity that the solar industry needs. Magnelis can go much lighter coating and still produce the same salt spray, same longevity, and it’s cheaper, faster, smarter.”

Wesler is the latest high-volume production facility for NX CoreFrame, and Nextpower plans to reach 6.5 GW of annual production through additional manufacturing partners across the country.

With an established steel frame design, the product just needs to find a place on panel manufacturer’s production lines. Origami Solar had been signing supply agreements with U.S. panel assemblers, an activity that has only increased with the Nextpower transition. Meydbray said the pitch for a domestic supply chain has become more attractive, especially with Nextpower’s established regional steel manufacturing network.

A machine treats steel coil as it travels along a factory line. Credit: Nextpower

“Steel strengthens the supply chain of domestic manufacturing facilities. When frames are bought overseas, they spend five, six weeks on the water before they actually arrive to the factory. [Panel companies] have about 2,000 pallets of frames in inventory when they source from overseas,” he said. “We’re able to provide actual just-in-time delivery to our factory clients. We can make deliveries every 48 hours. [They can save room] where they used to have pallets stacked up. There’s also the working capital of having stuff sit on the water for six weeks. Solar panel manufacturing is not a high-margin business, and working capital management is critical.”

Nextpower has inked supply agreements with many multi-gigawatt panel manufacturers in the United States, including Jinko, T1 and Thornova.

“This integration of Nextpower steel frames allows us to deliver high-performance modules tailored to the evolving U.S. market,” said Andrea Bodenhagen, global marketing director at Thornova Solar, which is establishing domestic solar cell manufacturing in Oklahoma. “By incorporating U.S.-made steel frames, we strengthen supply chain resilience while helping our customers maximize domestic content and improve project economics.”

With bonus incentives from the investment tax credit still in play when solar projects use domestic products, an American-made steel frame can make all the difference.

“When we see projects that have 100% domestic content on the tracker, we know that’s a perfect candidate for steel frames,” Meydbray said. “Steel frames get 6% domestic content credit. It’s the second cheapest percent after the tracker. A domestically made solar cell is a lot more expensive than an imported solar cell. The frame is the second-most cost-effective way to buy percentage points.”

The NX CoreFrame is universal and can be used with any brand’s mounting or tracking system, but there are advantages when the project can be designed more cohesively through one supplier. Nextpower has spent the last year expanding beyond mounting systems, now offering inverter, electrical balance of system and panel products.

“[Nextpower] is ambitious, but it’s not arrogant. It prioritizes the customer relationships it’s built over the years. We spent time with our customers asking where their pain points are and expanded the business accordingly …  the company that makes the thing the solar panel is mounted to is the same company that makes the frame that’s being mounted,” Meydbray said.

Kelly Pickerel was a long-time reporter with Solar Power World before leaving her post as editor in chief in 2026. You may find her on LinkedIn.

#5574. Court Approves $7.25B Settlement With Bayer Over Claims Roundup Caused Cancer

— Some attorneys for patients contend the settlement payouts are too small

Agrochemical maker Bayer will pay up to $7.25 billion over two decades to resolve thousands of claims from U.S. cancer patients asserting the company should have warned people that its popular weedkiller Roundup could cause lymphoma, according to a settlement approved Friday by a Missouri judge.

The nationwide settlement applies both to Roundup users who already are ill and those who become diagnosed with non-Hodgkin lymphoma in future years. Participants could get an average of between $10,000 and $165,000, depending on how they used the product, how old they were when diagnosed, and the severity of their illness.

For Germany-based Bayer, which denies that Roundup causes cancer, the settlement provides some financial certainty against mounting litigation costs that have led it to contemplate pulling the product from U.S. agricultural markets.

Litigants also faced uncertainty. While the settlement was pending, the U.S. Supreme Court undercut one of the central arguments in the Roundup lawsuits. The high court ruled in June that Bayer cannot be sued in state courts on claims it failed to warn about cancer, because federal regulations don't require a cancer warning label and states are forbidden from imposing additional labeling requirements.

Though some attorneys for cancer patients contend the settlement payouts are too small, others argue the deal is better than getting nothing -- which became a heightened likelihood after the Supreme Court ruling.

In a written judgment granting approval, St. Louis Circuit Judge Timothy Boyer said the average individual payouts are "fair and adequate" and the total dollar amount is "among the largest in the history of mass tort personal-injury class action settlements."

Bayer CEO Bill Anderson said in a statement that the court-approved settlement is "a decisive milestone in the company's strategy to mitigate the risks of both pending and future Roundup litigation."

Roundup Has Been Used on Weeds for Over 50 Years

Missouri-based Monsanto debuted Roundup weedkiller in 1974 with the chemical glyphosate as its active ingredient. The product quickly became one of the most widely used herbicides in agriculture. Roundup is designed to be used with genetically modified seeds that can resist the weedkiller's deadly effect, thus allowing farmers to produce more while conserving the soil by tilling it less.

The product also is commonly applied on lawns and gardens.

Bayer added Roundup to its portfolio when it acquired Monsanto in 2018. With it came a growing number of lawsuits alleging glyphosate causes non-Hodgkin lymphoma. But that remains in dispute. The World Health Organization's International Agency for Research on Cancer classified the chemical as "probably carcinogenic" in 2015. The Environmental Protection Agency has determined that it's not likely to cause cancer in humans when used as directed.

About 200,000 Roundup-related claims have been made against Bayer, many coming from people who used it on residential lawns and gardens. As a result, Bayer has removed glyphosate from its new versions of Roundup sold for residential markets.

The lawsuits have produced mixed results. Only a couple dozen cases have gone to jurors, some siding with Bayer and others with plaintiffs. In one case, a Georgia jury awarded plaintiffs $2.1 billion.

In the first Roundup verdict since the Supreme Court's precedent-setting ruling, a Missouri jury in October sided with Bayer in a case alleging design defects, negligence, and fraud. The verdict "should send a strong signal" that plaintiffs "will face significant challenges" pursuing Roundup-related cancer claims in the future, said Bill Dodero, Bayer's U.S. general counsel and global litigation leader.

#5573. Every Car Brand That Still Makes Motorcycles

Though it may surprise many, some car manufacturers also make great bikes. Honda is one of the best examples, starting as a motorized bicycle manufacturer in the '50s before branching out into automobiles. It continues to make two-wheelers today. BMW actually has an even longer history in the bike industry than Honda, since its maiden production bike, the R 32, hit the roads way back in 1923, and the Bavarian brand still makes products such as the R 1300 GS and S 1000 RR today.

Then there's Suzuki, which has a penchant for building some of the world's fastest bikes (such as the Hayabusa), plus the Chinese duo Great Wall Motor and Lifan. All of these manufacturers continue to sell cars alongside bikes. We should probably also mention that Audi has owned the celebrated motorcycle brand Ducati since 2012, though it doesn't make bikes under its own banner, so we've excluded it from this list.

Yep, it's a pretty busy operation over at Honda headquarters. Beyond plotting a racier Civic Type R for the U.S., among many other automotive projects, Honda also remains the world's biggest bike seller. With estimated annual sales of around 20 million bikes every year, distributed through roughly 30,000 dealerships around the world, it is still very much a focus for Honda.

The brand sells everything from scooters to adventure bikes, cruisers, high-end sports bikes, and touring bikes. Among its current touring bike offerings is the six-cylinder Gold Wing Tour, which features a double-wishbone front suspension and a dual-clutch transmission. It also gets a 5.5-gallon fuel tank and smartphone mirroring, so it's not quite as impractical as you might think. It isn't cheap, though, with prices starting from $30k for the base model. That's slightly more than what you'd pay for a Honda Civic Hybrid.

The German brand, by comparison, sold 202,563 motorbikes in 2025. Though it may seem puny next to Honda, that's still a very respectable figure, particularly for a brand that places far more of its eggs in the automotive basket. BMW is working on a whole new fleet of next-gen cars, including the production version of the 1000-hp M Concept Neue Klasse.

One of BMW's flagship bikes at present is the 2027 M 1000 RS, which starts at $39,555 with destination. Not only does it look radical, but it goes like a bat out of hell thanks to its 205-hp inline-4 and its mere 432 pounds of curb weight. That combination allows for a 0-124 mph time of just 7.2 seconds, the prospect of which generates more than a few shivers. BMW will limit production to just 1,000 units globally, so if you want your backside perched on that gorgeous Alcantara seat, you should probably act fast. We wouldn't be surprised if it becomes one of those motorcycles with top-tier resale values, too.

While Suzuki's passenger cars may not be the most exciting (and rarely have they ever been), the Japanese brand has developed some maniacal race cars in the past, including the 1000-hp Escudo Pikes Peak. Some of that craze has trickled through to its motorcycle division, which sold over 2.2 million vehicles in 2025.

You can count the stunning GSX-10R as part of its 2027 lineup. This new performance bike adopts a refined version of the 1.0-liter long-stroke inline-4 engine that traces its roots back to the legendary GSX-R1000 K5 superbike. It's being billed as a multipurpose bike, however, said to be as comfortable on a daily commute as it would be blasting through a racetrack. Oh, and it gets a rowdy-lookin' stainless steel exhaust, which you can bet will sing a pretty good song.

You may not have heard of Lifan, because compared to some other Chinese brands, it is a relatively small-scale business — and has gone through some pretty rough financial issues over the last few years. It was founded in 1992, and by the mid-2000s, it was building around two million motorcycles per year and selling in 160 countries around the world. That's quite the ascension.

After being saved from bankruptcy by Geely in 2020, Lifan's automobile sector spiraled into a new entity that focuses on AI and vehicle technology, but its sub-brand, Lifan Motorcycle, is still very much active. Its lineup includes the X-Trial, a dirt bike with a single-cylinder four-stroke engine, as well as the V400, which looks like the sort of cruiser Arnold Schwarzenegger's T-800 would ride.

That might be because GWM's two-wheeler division (SOUO) is still in its relative infancy, having only launched its first-ever bike, the S2000, in 2024. But it's powered by a corking 2.0-liter flat-8 engine and supposedly gets the world's first three-tier double-wishbone front suspension. It looks the part too, with stubby front and rear sections, multiple exhausts flowing out of its sides, and thick brake discs. It also has a proper seat carved out behind the primary rider's bench, so whoever is sitting back there would probably be nice and relaxed at the end of a long ride.

GWM won't yet sell you one in the U.S., but the S2000 was cleared for European distribution earlier this year. If and when it does eventually launch here, customers will probably see it as a competitor to the Honda Goldwing because of how focused it is on long-distance cruises. That's down to a combination of its 6.86-gallon fuel tank and variety of infotainment features, including Apple CarPlay connectivity. So, reckon it would be a hit 'round these parts?

#5572. Ford Begins Producing Its Own LFP Batteries

Ford is now a battery producer. Yes, it’s using battery technology licensed from CATL, but it is now producing EV batteries it is going to be using in its own vehicles. In fact, it is reportedly doing so in a really fast, advanced way. “The BlueOval Battery Park Michigan assembly lines can deliver 40 cells every minute, making it among the fastest LFP prismatic battery assembly lines outside of China.2 That pace helps drive down battery costs, and the facility has capacity for more. But speed must go hand in hand with quality,” Charles Poon, vice president of hardware engineering at Ford, writes.

“The first lithium iron phosphate (LFP) prismatic battery cells are rolling off the line at BlueOval Battery Park Michigan and headed to Louisville Assembly Plant to power Ford Fathom prototypes. With this milestone, Ford is now in the battery business and will be the first to deliver LFP batteries in the U.S. for mainstream consumer automotive use,” Poon writes. He goes on to explain further in a recent article why Ford chose to produce LFP battery cells and a bit on how it is doing so. Here’s a bit more from that article:

“The shipment caps years of work by the team that designed, built, and launched the plant on schedule. Ford now delivers cells at a plant it owns and operates, building expertise to support more affordable electric vehicles (EVs) and Ford Energy’s battery energy storage business in Kentucky.

“Why Start with LFP Prismatic for Fathom?

“When Alan Clarke, vice president, Advanced Development Projects, first joined the development team that created the Universal Electric Vehicle Platform, he issued a clear challenge: find an efficient LFP prismatic battery. The goal was to make EVs significantly more affordable.

“We talked to battery companies around the world. We wanted a technology that was proven in the field, ready to manufacture at scale, and suited to what we were building. That led us to the world’s leading battery manufacturer, CATL, whose batteries power 30 million vehicles across the globe.

“A battery is one of the biggest contributors to an EV’s cost and weight. So, we asked: How do we get more out of the battery without simply adding more cells? Our answer was efficiency over excess.

“LFP chemistry uses no nickel or cobalt, which helps hold down costs. It also holds up over years of charging and use. That durability is valuable as we give customers ways to use their battery beyond driving. For example, AC bidirectional power lets a vehicle receive electricity from a properly equipped home and supply power back to it.1

“This milestone is about more than shipping our first cells. We’re building the expertise to produce high-quality batteries at scale — for more affordable EVs and a new energy storage business.”

It looks like a serious effort and serious progress toward making more affordable, more competitive electric cars at Ford. The aim has been to get as good as making electric cars as Chinese companies are. How effective and successful will this new path be for Ford? We’ll see.

#5571. Why Gas Pumps Can't Pump Any Faster Than 10 Gallons A Minute

You know why fuel tanks shouldn't run low, so you head to your friendly neighborhood gas station. Let's hope you're not in a hurry, though, because sometimes gas pump speeds feel not so speedy at all. There's a reason for that. Several, in fact, and, sadly, none we can control.

The most likely cause is a mechanical issue, and it's "pick your part": the dispenser leak detector tripped, the fuel pump is clogged, the equipment is old, and so on. But before any fuel is dispensed, the flow has already been set to no faster than 10 gallons per minute (gpm). That's due to a federal rule dating back to the 1900s, as the kids would say. Or 1993 in adult years.

The purpose of establishing a fuel-dispensing rate was to improve performance of onboard vapor recovery systems as well as reduce the chances of spillage during refueling. This doesn't apply to every gas station across the U.S., however. Commercial facilities such as truck stops are exempt, because volume (and sales) matter.

The rule went into effect in 1996, and the 10 gpm figure isn't a stab in the gas tank dark. In a technical report released in 1987, the EPA stated that an investigation conducted by the American Petroleum Institute (API) found dispensing rates of the time were generally between 6 and 12 gpm, but that the average hovered around 8 to 10 gpm.

The main contributing factors to the varied rates was equipment type and age. Older and smaller stations likely used a suction pump, which kept rates around 8 to 9 gpm. Newer and high-volume stations were using submersible turbine pumps that delivered rates of 10 to 12 gpm. There were outliers. The API found rates as low as 6.5 gpm at full-service stations, while a self-serve location operated at high as 15 gpm.

In the end, the 10-gallon figure was settled upon partly because it matched the higher end of the existing dispensing-rate scale. Another consideration supporting the 10 spot was to ensure vehicle certification testing and dispensing rates remained compatible. Because fuel pumps were already delivering at 10 gpm, the vapor capture system of future vehicles would have little issue managing that speed. It was also a safeguard against splashback incidents.

According to the EPA, fueling facilities that exclusively cater to boats, airplanes, and heavy-duty vehicles (that is, big rigs) are exempt from the slow-flow rule. For commercial fuel centers, pumping at a 10-gpm rate makes little sense.

Semi-trucks have fuel tanks varying in size from 100 to 150 gallons, and some carry two of those. For everyday commuter cars and trucks, 10 gpm means a couple minutes to fill-up, at most. Even the gargantuan 50-gallon gas tub of a Ram 2500/3500 Heavy Duty will be topped off faster than your bathroom break.

Can you imagine filling up a big rig's reservoir at 10 gallons per minute, though? Who has the time when you're long-hauling large loads? Instead, truck stops are equipped with high-flow dispensers capable of pumping up to 50 gpm. Otherwise, you're looking at refueling bottlenecks for truckers and lost revenue for rest stop operators. And it won't matter if you're driving the world's fastest semi-truck if you're stuck waiting at the pump or in line to wait.

#5569. Alzheimer's Test Results Delivered Digitally Didn't Increase Distress

— Anxiety and knowledge scores also met noninferiority versus telehealth in randomized trial

Getting Alzheimer's genetic and biomarker results through a digital tool was noninferior to receiving the information during a telehealth appointment, the randomized eSMARTER trial showed.

One to 7 days after APOE results were disclosed, scores on measures of anxiety, disease-specific distress, and disease-related knowledge met noninferiority whether patients were randomized to a digital platform or to a clinician-mediated telehealth session, said Jessica Langbaum, PhD, of the Banner Alzheimer's Institute in Phoenix, at the Clinical Trials on Alzheimer's Disease conference in Boston.

Similar results emerged for phosphorylated tau 217 (p-tau217) disclosure, Langbaum reported. These findings were also published in JAMA Neurology.

"We found that learning Alzheimer's genetic and biomarker information from a digital platform is no less effective -- in other words, no worse than -- receiving this information from a clinician telehealth visit," Langbaum said.

"This is important given that most patients learn their test results from a report in their patient portal -- often a static report or PDF, with limited to no additional information or context about their results and what they mean -- and have to wait until their scheduled follow-up appointment to speak with their clinician," she pointed out.

Under the 21st Century Cures Act, most medical test results, including Alzheimer's genetic and biomarker findings, must be made available to patients immediately. Previous research has shown that disclosing Alzheimer's biomarker data is safe and generally well tolerated, but in those studies clinicians led the disclosure, either in person or by telehealth.

"eSMARTER is the first randomized trial to examine whether learning Alzheimer's genetic results via a patient-centered digital platform is noninferior to a telehealth visit with a study clinician," Langbaum said. "The study then offered participants the option to learn their plasma p-tau217 results via the same method -- digital platform or telehealth visit -- again examining differences in anxiety, distress, and knowledge."

The eSMARTER trial included participants ages 60 to 80 who had previously had APOE genotyping (without disclosure) through the GeneMatch research enrollment program, passed psychological screening, and had internet access. Participants had a mean age of 68 and a mean Montreal Cognitive Assessment (MoCA) score of 19. Approximately 36% had MoCA scores below 19, a cutoff commonly used for cognitive impairment, and most participants were women (67%).

The study randomized 216 participants to clinician-mediated APOE disclosure and 435 to digital disclosure. Genetic test results were revealed first, and after 6 months, participants could choose p-tau217 disclosure. All participants were offered optional digital educational materials about APOE and p-tau217 to review before the disclosure sessions.

Of 651 participants who received APOE disclosure, most were APOE4 carriers (58% heterozygotes, 10% homozygotes, and 32% noncarriers).

There were no overall differences in Alzheimer's-related knowledge, symptoms of anxiety, or disease-specific distress between the digital and telehealth arms after APOE results were received.

Among APOE4 carriers, three differences emerged:

The eSMARTER study shows that digital disclosure is an acceptable alternative to telehealth, Langbaum observed. The results suggest that a scalable platform could be developed "to plug into patient portals to help patients understand and contextualize their test results," she said. "We envision that these platforms can be used by clinicians and health systems to better support patients to navigate complex health information."

#5568. How Truck Aerodynamics Impact The Price Of Shipping

Paying higher prices can be a real drag, and the relationship also goes in the other direction: Drag — of the aerodynamic variety — can make a real impact on higher prices, too. The basic story starts with the fact that trucks continue to do most of the heavy lifting when it comes to moving stuff from one place to another. In the United States alone, they transport about 65% of all freight as measured by weight, according to Bureau of Transportation Statistics data.

Now, what they charge is based on a lot of different factors, but one of them is the need to cover their own operating costs. Next, we'll note that fuel costs are among the biggest chunks of a trucking company's expenses, and, as you might have noticed, those are way up. In fact, the cost of diesel has jumped $2.29 per gallon since this time last year. With modern semis able to manage an average of 7.43 mpg, according to the American Transportation Research Institute, that works out to an increase of more than $0.30 per mile. But the point is that anything affecting fuel expenses will affect overall operating costs, which will affect the price of shipping. And a key variable when it comes to fuel costs is fuel consumption. Here's where achieving a low drag coefficient actually comes into play.

A typical heavy truck, traveling at 70 mph, spends about 65% of its energy simply to overcome wind resistance, according to research from Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory. This means an improvement in aerodynamics results in less energy needed to fight drag and less fuel needed to make that energy. Connecting the dots takes us from spending less on fuel back along the chain until we get to the part where shipping companies set their prices.

With all that in mind, today's trucks can further rely on a wide range of aero upgrades to help chip away at fuel costs. For example, we previously mentioned the tiny airtabs that can improve not only fuel efficiency, but also performance and safety for semi trucks. Yet there are also plenty of other new aerodynamic aids to help fight high fuel prices. These generally address three important areas of the truck/trailer package where improvements can be most effective. The first is the gap between the rear of the truck cab and the front of the trailer. Here, truck roof fairings and side extenders help keep air out of the gap.

For the tractor, roof fairings can be especially effective, and when combined with components like cab extenders, side fairings, and a front bumper dam, they can boost fuel economy by up to 15%, according to EPA. On the other hand, for trailers — responsible for about 50% of the overall drag on a Class 8 tractor/trailer traveling on the highway — skirts have become far and away the most common choice. These can achieve 5% or more in fuel savings as verified by EPA's SmartWay program.

Moreover, the North American Council for Freight Efficiency (NACFE) recommends that truckers focus on overall aero packages, not just single components. There are some differences of opinion as regards what those bundles should include, however. SmartWay testing indicates trailer skirts combined with tail upgrades can help folks realize 9% or more in fuel savings — yet NACFE reports that the maintenance challenges of tail devices currently prevent them from being recommended.

→ Baca artikel asli

#5567. 5 Of Today's Best Exotic SUVs, Ranked By Horsepower

Like it or not, building a performance SUV is almost an obligatory part of being an exotic car manufacturer right now. Pretty much every major supercar brand has one. Even McLaren, which once swore that it would never make an SUV, is officially working on an SUV. They're huge when it comes to helping these brands keep the lights on, and still usually really flippin' fast.

In a straight line from a dead stop, most aren't that far off from their "real" supercar stablemates. Furthermore, in the case of new, four-digit horsepower, all-electric ones, they might even beat some actual supercars to the finish line. We've assembled five of today's best and most notable exotic SUVs from brands like Ferrari, Lamborghini, and Porsche, ranking them by pure horsepower. And guess what? There's a trend that, appropriately perhaps, purists are going to absolutely hate: The fewer cylinders there are, the more power there is.

Case in point: the Ferrari Purosangue is simultaneously the SUV on this list with the most cylinders and the one that makes the least power. We let that slide on sheer charisma, though, because what it's working with is a naturally aspirated 6.5-liter V12 — no turbos, no hybrid motors, just 12 cylinders of practical Italian fury.

It is weirdly one of only two cars in Ferrari's lineup that still uses a V12 (the other is the 12Cilindri grand tourer), but its mere 715 horses puts it on the back foot in terms of power compared to pretty much all of its chief competitors. That said, it's likely the best-sounding car of this group, having a redline of 8,250 rpm. Ferrari quotes a zero-to-62 mph time of 3.3 seconds and a top speed over 193 mph. It gets coach doors (the rears open backward) and starts at around $440,000.

Coming in above the Ferrari in terms of horsepower by the thinnest of margins is the 717-hp Aston Martin DBX S. The most powerful variant (so far) of Aston's SUV makes 20 more mechanical hp than the 697-hp DBX707 (the number in that car's name referred to metric hp, not mechanical), while weighing almost 104 pounds less thanks in part to a center-of-gravity-lowering carbon fiber roof.

That power comes from a 4.0-liter twin-turbo V8 borrowed from Mercedes-AMG, although Aston says some turbo tech, such as the bigger compressor wheel diameters, are taken from its own Valhalla supercar. Zero to 62 mph takes 3.3 seconds, and it tops out at 193, which are both identical to the Purosangue. Coincidence? Highly unlikely. When we drove it last year, we wrote that it "might be the best-driving SUV on sale." The DBX S starts at $274,500, but considering the one we tested had a final invoice of more than 400 grand, "starting prices" are sort of academic in this neighborhood of the market.

Given Audi's ownership of Lamborghini, it's perhaps not surprising that the first electrified entry on this list is the Urus SE Performante. Those familiar with Lambo nomenclature will already know that this is the pumped-up version of the plug-in hybrid version of the brand's very popular SUV. Like the Aston Martin DBX, this car uses a 4.0-liter twin-turbo V8, albeit borrowed and adapted from its parent company, Audi. The SE part of the equation adds a hybrid electric motor that results in a total of 801 horsepower.

Comically, however, it's not any quicker than its un-electrified rivals and just barely faster than its Aston Martin counterpart. Electricity is mighty, but also heavy. Zero to 62 mph takes 3.3 seconds (exactly the same as the DBX S and Purosangue). In comparison, the Urus SE Performante's 312 km/h (194 mph rounded) top speed is one mile higher than the DBX S' 193 mph, but Ferrari is more vague with reporting the Purosangue's top speed, simply listing the SUV as capable of exceeding 310k m/h (193 mph rounded). The Urus SE Performante's starting price has not yet been confirmed.

When it comes to raw horsepower and speed, though, gas-powered entries are sort of fighting for silver these days because all-electric offerings like the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric are putting out horsepower figures with commas in 'em. This specific Porsche makes 1,139 horsepower from two electric motors. Zero to 60 mph takes 2.4 seconds (almost an entire second quicker than its gas rivals), but top speed is just 162 mph (more than 30 mph down from internal combustion), which speaks to what performance EVs are good at and what they aren't. The electric Cayenne Turbo features nifty side aeroblades that deploy outward to reduce drag.

In the more exotic-looking Coupe form, this car starts at $170,350, magnitudes less than the other vehicles we've seen so far, but that likely speaks more to the Porsche's "exoticness" than abilities. To those who question whether Porsche even qualifies as an "exotic" automaker, though: if a 1,139-hp car with moving sideblades and optional gold wheels isn't an "exotic SUV," I don't know what is.

In a similar vein, if the Urus with its VW Touareg underpinnings is obviously an exotic SUV, then the new Mercedes-AMG GT 63 SUV and its relation to cars like the old GT Black Series should count, too. It definitely counts on accelerative merit: three axial flux electric motors drawing energy from a Formula 1-derived 800-volt battery deliver 1,153 horsepower. Zero to 60 takes 2.4 seconds with a one-foot rollout and 2.6 seconds without. Top speed stands at an electronically limited 168 mph, precisely 6 mph faster than the Porsche but still not in the same time zone as the 190-plus mph gas rivals.

The AMG GT SUV is one of those electric performance cars that mimics the sound of a gas car engine — specifically an AMG V8 — with simulated gear shifts and corresponding seat vibrations also present. As of this writing, the electric AMG GT SUV is too new for us to have driven it yet, but we recently had a go in the sedan and found it mind-blowingly good. The SUV, then, shouldn't be far behind. Mercedes-AMG's GT SUV pricing has not yet been confirmed for the U.S.

#5566. The Insurance Affordability Crisis: Risks, Resilience, and Solutions

NRDC resources on insurance affordability, market availability, climate risk, resilience investments, and strategies to protect homeowners, businesses, and communities.

For property owners, insurance provides a critical financial safety net that protects homes, businesses, and other assets from unexpected losses, helping owners recover and rebuild when disasters occur. Beyond individual protection, insurance is fundamental to the resilience of communities and local economies, enabling faster recovery, supporting continued investment, and reducing the long-term financial impacts of shocks. As risks become more frequent, severe, and costly, access to affordable, available, and reliable insurance is increasingly essential, not only for safeguarding property owners but also for maintaining overall property affordability, economic stability, and the resilience and prosperity of the communities in which they live and operate.

The frequency and severity of losses are increasing faster than insurers can sustainably price and absorb them, leading to higher premiums, reduced coverage availability, and, in some cases, the withdrawal of insurers from certain markets. This trend is eroding the affordability and accessibility of insurance, increasing the total cost of property ownership, and placing additional pressure on housing and business affordability while leaving individuals, businesses, and communities more physically and financially exposed.

However, the insurance sector is increasingly recognized as a key component of resilience, incentivizing risk reduction through pricing and support for adaptation measures that can help moderate long-term insurance costs. When functioning effectively, insurance enables communities to not only recover from shocks but also to better prepare for future risks while preserving access to coverage.

Linked below are reports, expert blogs, and other resources that examine the insurability crisis and how insurance can play a key role in advancing housing resilience and adaptation.

NRDC’s director of insurance and financial risk, Alfonso Pating, breaks down how homeowner’s insurance works and why climate change is making it unaffordable.

More reports and resources on this topic here.

Article originally published on NRDC website.

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5565. 7 Cars With The Smallest Trunks You Can Imagine

No single car can deliver everything that every driver will ever want from it, and they all come with at least some compromises. Performance cars often aren't that fuel efficient, huge trucks and SUVs with impressive towing capabilities are not the most nimble for around town, and — as this list will prove — a variety of city and sports cars also need to compromise on trunk space, in order to prioritize other key traits.

That doesn't make them inherently bad cars, they just excel in other areas. The list includes various city-slick models like the Scion iQ and Renault Twizy – if these cars were equipped with cavernous trunks, then the smallest parking spots and tightest city streets would likely be best avoided. Similarly, sports and supercars, like the Fiat X1/9 and Audi R8 featured below, sport mid-engine layouts, which naturally make incorporating decent trunk space quite a tricky ordeal.

For this list, we've singled out seven cars with laughably small trunks. If you know of smaller, feel free to drop them in the comments below, although it's probably important to point out that we've deliberately avoided models with zero trunk space — so, no BAC Mono or Morgan Plus 4 models to be found here. Likewise, not all trunk capacities are listed by automakers, so any models we could not reliably verify — such as the Veyron Grand Sport Vitesse's supposed 1.3-cubic-foot trunk — have also been avoided. With that aside, here is our selection of seven notoriously impractical trunks and the cars they belong to, listed in size order, with the largest featured first.

The first entry is an Italian classic that does things differently. Whereas most mid-engine sports cars hailing from Italy boast impressive engines and lofty outputs, the Fiat X1/9 instead makes do with a tiny inline-4. It sports just a 1.3-liter capacity, and power peaks at 67 horses, with 68 pound-feet of torque for good measure. Even with a 2,200-pound curb weight, it still takes around 15 seconds for the little X1/9 to crack 60 mph from a standstill.

As those figures suggest, this is a small car — its wheelbase is just 86.7 inches, which is over 4 inches shorter than that of the Miata below. As a result, there isn't a whole heap of room left for a considerable trunk — the X1/9's frunk provides owners just 5.1 cubic-feet of space.

However, here's where the X1/9 actually impresses, as, despite the diminutive proportions and mid-engine configuration, it actually manages to incorporate a traditional trunk, too. Here lies 4.1 cubic-feet of storage space, so in total, the tiny Fiat actually provides 9.2 cubes — very impressive, considering the space available in other mid-engine efforts. That frunk is typically used as stowage space for the removable targa roof panel, but, of course, with the roof in place or left at home, it could be used for whatever.

This list covers many city-centric little runabouts and exotic supercars, but here instead, we focus on something that lies somewhere between the two. Mazda's timeless MX-5 Miata is a back-to-basics sports car that refuses to follow the status-quo. While other sports cars have resorted to larger dimensions, heftier curb weights, turbocharged engines, and higher engine outputs, Mazda instead continues to focus on simple driving pleasures.

That's perfect for drivers chasing a fun drop-top to enjoy on a local back road, but it does mean that, once again, trunk space takes a back seat on the priorities list. At least, due to Mazda adopting a traditional front-engine and rear-wheel drive configuration, the Miata sports a proper trunk. It only offers 4.6 cubic-feet of actual space, though, so it's never going to be a great touring companion — at least not without owners having to make some sort of packing compromise or opting for an external luggage rack. The cabin isn't particularly clever in terms of extra storage either, with no door compartments to be seen and no traditional glove box.

Interestingly, the current ND-generation MX-5 Miata sports less luggage space than its predecessor, so Mazda clearly did not feel that trunk space had ever been much of a sticking point with Miata buyers in the past. In fact, we recently advised you all to buy a Miata and said that you'd likely never regret it, so we also feel the sports car's limited storage space is nothing too concerning either.

Lamborghini is the only automaker to appear twice on this list, although we doubt being called out for limited cargo space is going to damage the Italian supercar manufacturer's brand image all too much. The Temerario continues the baby supercar bloodline, following on from the Huracan and Gallardo before it, and unlike the Aventador-based Countach further down your page, it's an all-new and fresh design.

There is a pattern emerging here, too, as much like the Countach revival, the Temerario is another mid-engine supercar, and another to sport hybridization technology. Naturally, that leaves no space for a traditional trunk, so it offers a frunk as its primary storage solution. It also matches the capability of later Audi R8 Coupe models, with only 4 cubic-feet of space given at the front end. That's perhaps enough for a few backpacks for a weekend getaway, but certainly not ideal for longer trips that may require multiple larger bags.

It doesn't matter how you spec a Temerario, it's never going to be a great grand-tourer with that little in the way of storage space. An Urus or front-engined model from another exotic brand would be the better bet there, but at least Lamborghini provides owners with enough space to stash away a few essentials for weekends away.

Audi had never made anything that truly verged on being a supercar, so when it pulled up in the late 2000s with a true 911 competitor, with a naturally aspirated and midship 4.2-liter V8, 6-speed manual, and quattro all-wheel drive, the motoring world sat up and paid attention. By all accounts, it was a sublime car, although, thanks to its mid-engine configuration, cargo space has always been at a premium.

Second-generation cars, which ran from the 2016 model year until Audi's supercar became one of the many nameplates that got the axe in 2024, came with a total of 8 cubic-feet of storage space behind the seats, while the physical frunk itself sports 4 cubic-feet. As for those first-gen models, the total frunk space matches that of the Toyota iQ, at only 3.5 cubic-feet. There is no mention of classifying the space behind seats as official stowage space here, so perhaps doing so was just a smart marketing trick of the later cars to make the R8 look like a more complete 911 rival. Either way, there is surely space for a pair of shades or a short novel in the cabin of first-gen models somewhere.

Moving back away from performance cars, the next entry on our list is another cleverly packaged city car. The Scion iQ, or Toyota iQ, as it was known in other markets, was a dinky little four-seater, the first four-seater, in fact, to sit at less than 3 meters (9.8 feet) in length, although it was certainly more conventional in its approach than the tandem-seat Twizy, detailed below. In truth, the Scion iQ was an excellent little car that nobody wanted to buy, produced from the 2012 through 2015 model years, and powered by a naturally aspirated 1.3-liter inline-4 engine.

While it did have the capacity to seat four, those rear seats were particularly restrictive and better treated as an additional storage area. Just as well, as the designated storage space in the trunk was equally as restrictive, at just 3.5 cubic-feet. While the little city car certainly has its fans, not everyone loves it: Consumer Reports called it "slow, noisy, and uncomfortable," in addition to "less-than-enjoyable to drive."

Still, it probably made good sense for those who commuted solely within packed city centers. Furthermore, if those buyers had far deeper pockets and wished for a more upscale ownership experience, they could always opt for the iQ's posher sibling, the Aston Martin Cygnet. The British luxury carmaker took the back-to-basics iQ, furnished it with a sublime interior and refinished the rear end, in addition to many other cosmetic and quality updates. It commanded around three times the price of the iQ, was never offered for sale in the U.S., and, although undoubtedly far nicer to sit in, it likely still made do with the same measly 3.5 cubic-feet of trunk space.

At the complete other end of the spectrum, cargo space isn't far removed from what the city-centric Scion iQ has to offer. To celebrate the initial 1971 release of its Countach, Lamborghini released a modern-day Countach built on the Aventador's chassis. A hybrid V12 powertrain pinched from the Sián FKP37 hybrid hypercar was selected as the motivator, and, as a tribute to the original prototype's LP-112 model designation, just 112 were assembled.

Cargo space likely was not at the forefront of the designer's mind, and neither was it a key priority for buyers either, we can safely assume, as the Countach LPI 800-4 offers just 2.2 cubic-feet of it. That's roughly enough room for two standard milk crates — great news for big milk drinkers, then, but not ideal for road trippers or those who fancy an exotic daily driver.

The Aventador Coupe, on which the Countach is based, manages to boast 5 cubic-feet of cargo space, so more than double, which might make the Countach's lack of available space seem tricky to understand. While Lamborghini (rightfully) doesn't feel the need to explain the Countach's cargo space, which is actually frunk space rather than trunk space, we can assume it's due to the Countach's differing lines and the fact it also boasts a hybrid system, whereas the Aventador is without electrification.

The Renault Twizy was not sold in the U.S., as it likely wouldn't sell well being such a diminutive electrified city runabout, and restrictions would render it pretty difficult to use anyway. And that's if it ever worked its way around the required crash tests in the first place. Having said that, Scoot Networks did temporarily employ a fleet of them around San Francisco for rental use, but that's not why it grabs the spotlight today.

Instead, it impresses — or rather disappoints — with its ludicrously compact trunk capacity. It offers just 31 liters of space, which, for reference, works out to around 1.09 cubic-feet. To put that into perspective, a standard milk crate measures up at 0.9 cubic-feet. So, the Twizy is ideal for grabbing a few gallons of milk, but that's about it.

In truth, it would be rude and missing the point somewhat to blast the Twizy over cargo space. It's designed to be as compact as possible, suitable for two occupants in tandem and, well, nothing else really. So, for city-center commuting and securing the last parking spot that nobody else dares to entertain, this quadricycle is absolutely ideal. Just be careful about the size of your packed lunch before heading out to work.

#5564. Plastic Surgeon Convicted of Killing Lawyer Is Sentenced to Death

— Tomasz Kosowski killed attorney Steven Cozzi in the bathroom of a law office

A judge in Florida this week sentenced a plastic surgeon to death for killing an attorney in a law office bathroom and then dumping his body in a trash bin in the Everglades, where it was never found.

Tomasz Kosowski, MD, killed attorney Steven Cozzi in the bathroom of Cozzi's law office in March 2023, moments after both participated in a conference call about a lengthy, acrimonious legal battle over medical billing, prosecutors said.

Prosecutors said Kosowski took the call from a Toyota pickup truck outside the office and that he had supplies to commit the killing, including trash bags, a syringe containing a paralyzing drug, and a wagon to haul the corpse out.

Investigators used cellphone records and surveillance video to track Kosowski to a remote area west of Miami on the Tamiami Trail, also known as U.S. Highway 41. That's where they believe Kosowski tossed the body into a dumpster that was eventually emptied by a garbage truck. The driver noticed an unusually "vile" smell at the stop, authorities said.

Trash from the route is typically hauled to a Collier County landfill, but authorities who searched the property for Cozzi's body said the facility routinely compacts its trash, making recovery efforts nearly impossible. In addition, investigators said, Cozzi's blood and DNA were found in the law office bathroom and in the garage at Kosowski's home.

Kosowski was convicted in September, following a trial in which he represented himself and presented no defense beyond making a closing argument in which he said prosecutors had not proved their case.

Pinellas Circuit Judge Joseph Bulone was tasked with deciding whether to sentence Kosowski to death or to life in prison without the possibility of release.

Kosowski declined to present any mitigating evidence on his own behalf, but a special counsel appointed by the court highlighted his lack of criminal history and his medical work. A graduate of Dartmouth College medical school, Kosowski specialized in reconstructive breast surgery.

The judge acknowledged in court that there were several mitigating factors, but said none outweighed the aggravating factors -- most importantly, that the crime was committed in a "cold, calculating, and premeditated manner, without any pretense of moral or legal justification."

"The court concludes that the defendant, under the laws of the State of Florida, has forfeited his right to live," Bulone said.

Investigators said that when they arrested Kosowski, he had $280,000 in cash, masks, duct tape, firearms, a ballistic vest with "EMS" written on it, law enforcement patches, and a vial of succinylcholine, which is a paralyzing drug. A search of his home turned up about 200 guns, according to court documents.

Cozzi, meanwhile, seemingly disappeared without a trace. His keys, wallet, and cellphone remained on his law office desk and a work file was open on his computer. His husband never heard from him.

The missing lawyer represented a Dunedin, Florida-based medical practice that Kosowski alleged had shorted him thousands of dollars in billings and damaged his reputation as a doctor.

Bulone appointed the public defender's office to represent Kosowski in his appeals.

#5563. RMI’s Hydrogen Research Is Getting Better. Its Investment Advice Still Falls Short.

RMI has become much more realistic about hydrogen demand and competing technologies. That makes the gaps between its findings and its recommendations harder to excuse. Its latest work acknowledges that battery trucks generally have better economics than hydrogen fuel-cell trucks, that Europe needs far less hydrogen in 2030 than it originally targeted, and that low-emissions ammonia still costs substantially more than conventional ammonia. Several recommendations still seek public money to develop the very hydrogen markets the research has found difficult to justify.

I have been challenging this tension since January 2024. RMI matters because governments, investors and industrial companies use its research when deciding where to spend money. Its hydrogen program has made genuine analytical corrections. It has not made all of the accompanying strategic decisions.

Consider RMI’s February 2026 report on hydrogen trucking in India. The baseline comparisons find that fuel-cell trucks do not reach battery-electric truck ownership-cost parity through 2040, including the long-haul use case. The report also explores a much more favourable hydrogen price in an alternative sensitivity, which brings fuel-cell trucks closer to parity. Those are different findings, and the baseline matters to investment decisions today.

The report nevertheless recommends hydrogen vehicle subsidies, support for refuelling stations, production incentives and tax relief. It treats a parallel hydrogen-truck market as something to build. The case for spending public money on that market should start with evidence that a particular freight service cannot be served as well or better by batteries. The reported cost advantage of batteries does not disappear when the policy section begins.

The problem reaches beyond India. RMI’s 2025 US hydrogen report says battery trucks have greater market readiness and lower total cost of ownership, especially in the near term, yet calls for hydrogen distribution and refuelling infrastructure along freight corridors. The independent NACFE Run on Less ownership-cost work, developed within RMI’s freight research ecosystem, projects batteries becoming the lowest-cost option across its modelled duty cycles by 2035 without incentives. Hydrogen remains the most expensive option across nearly all of them. These findings support battery infrastructure as the default and a high evidentiary bar for hydrogen exceptions.

RMI already knows how to make appropriate distinctions elsewhere. Its 2026 steel research separates hydrogen used to reduce iron ore from hydrogen burned merely to supply process heat. In the illustrative plant, electric heat requires about 850 GWh a year, compared with 1,300 GWh of electricity to produce enough additional hydrogen for heating. That is the kind of direct service comparison that ought to govern recommendations throughout the organization.

Its new Green Ammonia Buyers Alliance also has a coherent industrial purpose. Fertiliser production already requires ammonia, commonly made with fossil-derived hydrogen. Purchasing lower-emissions ammonia to replace that supply addresses an existing industrial input. It does not require inventing a new energy market for ammonia combustion.

Europe shows how improved diagnosis can coexist with excessive policy ambition. RMI’s March 2025 recalibration reduced expected mandate-driven renewable hydrogen demand in 2030 to 2.2–2.8 million tons, far below the EU’s original 20-million-ton supply-and-import ambition. Its analysis identifies €13.9 billion in public support under existing mandates, plus €31.7 billion for an accelerated scenario. The combined €45.6 billion deserves a comparison with the other ways industry could obtain the same low-carbon products and services, including direct electrification and imported green materials.

That comparison changes the question for ammonia, steel and refining. A European fertiliser or chemical manufacturer needs ammonia, not necessarily hydrogen manufactured inside Europe. A steelmaker needs iron meeting its grade and emissions requirements. A mill needs process heat at specified temperatures. Governments can pay a premium to preserve domestic capacity for security or resilience, but the benefit must be valued and the public cost made explicit.

My November 2026 European hydrogen assessment applies that approach. In its central 2050 scenario, ammonia produced in Europe with on-site green hydrogen costs approximately €1,222 per ton. Qualifying imported green ammonia costs €745–755 per ton under the model’s assumptions. These are modelled values, not supplier bids. They demonstrate why an analysis that starts with hydrogen production targets can produce the wrong infrastructure plan.

RMI’s 2026 China green ammonia and methanol report acknowledges that green production costs remain two to three times conventional costs and that demand development lags supply ambitions. The report nevertheless discusses expanding ammonia and methanol as energy carriers, including ammonia for electricity. The relevant comparison for electricity is renewable generation with storage and other flexibility, not fossil-derived ammonia alone. RMI has not demonstrated that adding an ammonia combustion pathway wins that comparison.

This is a strategy problem as much as a modelling problem. Using Richard Rumelt’s framework, RMI’s diagnosis has improved, its guiding policy remains too committed to expanding hydrogen markets, and some coherent actions are missing. An economically unfavourable comparison should be able to end an infrastructure recommendation. Instead, it too often generates proposals for subsidies that would make the hydrogen route easier to sell.

RMI has the expertise to correct this. Its hydrogen recommendations should carry explicit alternative-technology comparisons, investment thresholds and exit criteria. Cross-program review should include the researchers measuring battery costs, industrial electrification, freight operations and grid flexibility. Where hydrogen is an essential chemical input or demonstrably the best means of delivering a service, support it. Where competing technologies win on cost and deployment readiness, stop trying to create demand through policy.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines RMI’s US, India, Europe and China reports side by side, including the differences between its findings and recommendations. It also links the original sources and the 2024 critique.

Michael Barnard has 1560 posts and counting. See all posts by Michael Barnard

#5562. Buick GSX Stage 1s Are Super Rare Muscle Cars, But They're Common Compared To Stage 2s

If you take a moment to think of some classic-era American muscle car brands, names like Chevrolet, Pontiac, and Dodge immediately come to mind, but "Buick" doesn't. Buick occupies a market segment just under luxurious Cadillac within the GM hierarchy of automakers, making athleticism something most wouldn't associate the brand with. Regardless, the marque was responsible for building one of the most torque-happy muscle cars of the classic era, with 510 pound-feet of torque on tap alongside 360 horsepower.

The model responsible is the 1970 Buick GSX Stage 1. At the time, the GSX was locked in direct battle with more well-known adversaries such as the Pontiac GTO and the Dodge Charger, but even when it was new, few knew about it. It was one of the rarest of muscle cars — only about 400 were produced in 1970. To boot, it became even rarer as time went on, with just 24 built in total for the 1972 model year.

However, "Stage 1" was only part of the GSX's performance story. There was also the incredibly fabled "Stage 2" iteration. Only a couple of fully fledged examples were ever produced  — making it one of the rarest Buick muscle cars to ever grace the pavement.

If you fancied a hot Buick muscle car in 1970, you could simply pop down to the dealership and buy something like the GS 350 or GS 455. Your most desirable choice, however, would have been the flagship Buick GSX, carrying a sticker price of about $4,880. It boasted the poise and presence of a tried-and-true muscle car with its black striping and lack of chrome trim — something rarely seen in a Buick of the time. The Stage 1 package was offered for just $199, adding 10 horsepower and things like a higher compression ratio at 10.5:1 versus the GSX's standard 10.0:1, a more aggressive camshaft, and larger valves.

If you took a walk back into the Buick dealer's parts department, though — and also happened to be a drag racer — you could build yourself an even more powerful GSX with Buick-bred components that were off the standard books. This marked the initiation method for procuring a Stage 2 kit, and it needed to be special-ordered through dealers, rather than from Buick itself. The package consisted of a litany of upgrades over the Stage 1 version. Because it wasn't available to the public at first, the Stage 2 upgrade went without an RPO code.

The Stage 2 kit carried things like an 11.0:1 compression ratio and 2-inch circular exhaust ports, allowing the engine to breathe much more efficiently. A gigantic Holley carburetor sporting an 850 cubic-foot-per-minute flow rate sat atop an Edelbrock intake manifold, and a set of high-strength forged pistons handled the engine's combustion.

All told, the Stage 2 GSX was thought to have roughly 100 more horsepower than Stage 1 models. To test its Stage 2 creation, Buick drummed up a pair of GSX prototypes outfitted with the complete kit, one of which was sent to Doug Jones and Dave Benisek — a pair of drag racers — for some stress testing, with the duo managing to achieve quarter-mile times under 11.7 seconds at trap speeds nearing 130 mph. Jones and Benisek continued to race their prototypical Stage 2 GSX throughout the 1970s, going undefeated during the National Hot Rod Association's 1972 season.

That very same year, Buick finally made the existence of its Stage 2 project public, letting regular customers special-order parts from the program. As for the rough number of Stage 2 kits built, it's thought to be just about 75 pairs, the vast majority of them produced in 1972. Sadly, looming changes to American emissions standards rendered the factory-sponsored Stage 2 program moot before it could get really off the ground.

1972 was a pivotal year for the classic American muscle car. Horsepower outputs had dropped dramatically by that time, and the GSX Stage 1 was no exception. While the 1970 model boasted 360 ponies, the '72 GSX Stage 1 made just 270. Fitting, then, that Buick's Stage 2 GSX dream would effectively flame out that year — right around the time emissions standards and rising fuel costs began to render the classic American muscle car a thing of the past.

#5561. Stellantis CEO More Bullish on EVs than Stellantis CEOs of Old, But Sings Same Boring Song

Stellantis has never been a bastion of EV enthusiasm and progress. Back in the day (about 10 years ago), Sergio Marchionne was a very openly, blatantly anti-EV CEO. He actually asked customers to not buy their first electric car, the Fiat 500e.

Stellantis is still dragging its feet on EVs in the United States and elsewhere, but the assumption at the top seems to have shifted to believing that the whole industry will transition over the EVs eventually. Just not today.

CEO Antonio Filosa recently said that the US would eventually embrace electric vehicles, but that EV adoption is just slower here than in other regions (where there’s a combination of much more model choice, more government support, more EV awareness, and not so many barriers at the dealer level).

Filosa notes that the share of new auto sales that are electrics is just 7% this year, about the same as five years ago. “It has been a bubble driven by regulation. ​But then, when the administration has decided to deregulate, the ⁠consumers have been preferring other powertrains,” he added. It’s hard to argue with such claims. There are barriers to more sales, as already noted, but Americans apparently haven’t stormed dealerships looking for EVs except in certain rare circumstances. The fact is: more vehicle options and probably better consumer awareness have led to countries across Asia, Europe, and South America rapidly increasing their EV adoption rates and catching up to or passing the US market.

“The level of magnitude and the speed of ​transition is going to be lower for sure than other regions,” Filosa predicted, but he said he “truly” believed the market would eventually boom in the US.

As others have noted in recent times, Filosa says the automobile market is becoming more regional again, less global. That may be the case, but it is also clear that some EV companies, most notably BYD, are rapidly expanding across the globe and helping to spur on much quicker EV adoption. The issue with the US is that it’s blocking such companies with severe 100% (and higher) tariffs. So, the US is getting no boost from these highly competitive new vehicles.

What we have seen — whether in Europe, China, Australia, or Ecuador — is that markets that have a lot of EV choice and that have automakers trying to sell a lot of EVs can, in fact, find buyers. So, it’s hard to believe the claim that consumers in the US just don’t want EVs. It seems more likely to me that we don’t have enough EV options, automakers don’t try very hard to sell their EVs if they don’t have to, and there are significant barriers to put in front of consumers when they go to car dealerships. Is Filosa right — that Americans just don’t want EVs that much yet without serious incentives from the government? Or is he just singing the same old disingenuous or wrong song in saying that Americans by and large aren’t ready for EVs yet?
