#5560. 2027 Audi Q7 And SQ7 Are, Like, Reeeeally Good

It may feel like the Audi Q7 has existed forever, and in the world of modern cars, it kinda has. The first-generation Audi Q7 debuted 20 years ago now, making a name for itself as a chicer and more car-like option among three-row luxury SUVs such as the Volvo XC90, Land Rover LR3, and Mercedes-Benz GL. The second-gen Q7 has been around for 10 years, and over its lifespan Ingolstadt's three-row SUV has faded into the background of the now much more competitive segment, so it's about time the Q7 got a good redesign.

Luckily for Audi, the third-gen 2027 Q7 and its performance-focused SQ7 sibling are two fantastically compelling options for the luxury SUV buyer to consider. If you need even more space than the Q7 offers, Audi also recently introduced the excellent new Q9 and SQ9, which thoroughly impressed me during my first drive review. My colleague Andy Kalmowitz accurately and eloquently described the Q9 as "a new-generation Q7 that took half a Viagra," so you'll see a lot of similar specs and conclusions in this review — like its longer sibling, the new Q7 is really damn good.

Full disclosure: Audi wanted me to drive its redesigned Q7 and SQ7 so badly that it flew me to California's wine country, put me up in a swanky hotel, and kept me fed for a couple of nights.

The third-generation Audi Q7 is 199.1 inches long, only 0.2 inches shorter than the outgoing model, but the new Q7 is 2.7 inches taller and 1.5 inches wider. Interior dimensions have only changed slightly as well, with the new one offering slightly more cargo space and second-row legroom, but slightly less front seat headroom and third-row legroom on paper. This all varies, though, since the second row of seats slide forward and back to allow for the best combination of space for your family's unique needs.

In standard Q7 form the SUV is powered by a twin-turbocharged 2.9-liter V6 that sends 429 horsepower and 442 pound-feet of torque through a slick-shifting eight-speed automatic transmission. From there, naturally, the power goes to all four wheels through a new Quattro all-wheel-drive system with a limited-slip center differential. This standard powertrain is capable of a 0-to-60 time of 4.8 seconds, which Audi claims is the quickest in the segment, and in practice it's plenty powerful. That's a lot of standard performance baked into the Q7's base price of $72,295.

There's also a lot of luxurious features included as standard at that price, including an opening panoramic sunroof, 14-way power front sports seats with four-way lumbar and memory, Audi's array of three screens, LED lights with three different daytime running light designs, natural wood inlays, three-zone automatic climate control, a power tailgate, power-folding third-row seats, a surround-view camera system, and leather upholstery.

My test car was a top-trim Q7 Prestige with a price of $91,435, which included the 21-inch wheel package, the 4D audio system, a trailer hitch, and second-row captains chairs.

The SQ7, like the SQ9, is powered by a reworked twin-turbocharged 4.0-liter V8 that sends 591 horsepower and 590 lb-ft of torque through an eight-speed automatic transmission to a new rear-biased Quattro system with an electronically locking rear differential. If that sounds like a lot of power to you, you're wrong. I think it's the perfect amount of power for a three-row family SUV, and it's a valuable safety feature to be able to complete the 0-to-60 sprint in an Audi-claimed 3.7 seconds. You know, just in case.

All that power comes with a base price of $100,095, but that includes some features that are only found on upper trim levels of the Q7, including Audi's new hands-free highway driving assistant, a Bang & Olufsen 3D stereo, Nappa leather ventilated and massaging front seats, adaptive air suspension, all-wheel steering, and heated outboard second-row seats.

Like the Q9, both of these smaller SUVs can also be optioned with a Luxury package that includes automatic doors, digital OLED tail lights, eight digital DRL signatures, and an illuminated grille to luxe things up. Both are also offered with Audi's Digital Matrix LED headlights that are finally allowed in the U.S.; these will allow drivers to leave their high beams active while the car identifies and selectively dims some of the lighting elements in order to prevent blinding other cars, and it blocks the beam from hitting street signs that could reflect the beam and dazzle the driver. My test car was a decked-out SQ7 with the 23-inch wheel package, 4D audio, a trailer hitch, second-row captain's chairs, and the Luxury, Executive, Vision, and S Sport packages. It had a total price of $117,085.

During my drive on the winding roads around California's wine country, through the redwood forests and along the rocky northern coast, the new Q7 and SQ7 revealed themselves as excellent luxury SUVs. The Q7's base powertrain, if you can call it base, was more than punchy enough to get the nearly 5,200-pound three-row SUV up to freeway speeds lickety-split. Audi's 4.8-second 0-to-60 claim felt almost like an underestimation to the butts of myself and my co-driver, and that estimation is also 0.7 seconds quicker than the outgoing V6-powered Q7, so there's a lot of extra shove hiding under the more upright hood of the new car.

Activating launch control unlocks the most exhilarating acceleration, and it's easily done. Just press the traction control button once and tap the shifter down to engage S mode, then stomp on the brake pedal, floor the gas, and release the brakes.

Twisty roads may not appeal to most three-row SUV drivers, but they will be a delight to anyone who drives a new Audi Q7 because it is one well sorted driving machine. Whether in the default Balanced drive mode or the zestiest Dynamic mode, the Q7 was not just capable but felt happy to dance through the damp curves and swerves that wound us through the downright verdant Redwoods State Park and along the breathtaking cliffs of Cape Horn.

Some of the credit for my test Q7's excellent driving dynamics must go to its top-tier Prestige trim level, which equips the Q7 with one of the most natural feeling and effective all-wheel-steering systems I've ever experienced. It made the Q7 feel significantly more agile than its girth leads one to believe when getting frisky in the twisties, and it made low-speed maneuvering a breeze, even in the tight, small-town parking lots of Ukiah, California.

The Q7 Prestige trim also includes front sport seats with power adjustable bolsters, which provided prestigious levels of comfort over the course of my seven-hour long drive day. They're actually the same seats that come standard on all SQ7s, and they also happen to have one of the most effective massage functions of any car on the road.

As a whole, the Q7 provided an experience that was befitting of a $90,000 luxury SUV, with exceptional comfort, a stress-free driving experience, and a smooth and gutsy powertrain. The only two demerits that could potentially put luxury buyers off are a taut ride quality that doesn't isolate passengers from roadway imperfections quite as much as other comfier SUVs, and a bit more road noise than some competitors. Both of those factors could have been down to the large wheels and tires on my test car, though.

Much the same as its bigger SQ9 sibling, the SQ7 is mind-bendingly quick and adept at bombing down a back road. The SQ7's twin-turbo V8 sounds fantastic from both outside and inside the cabin, where its deep bellow and occasional off-throttle burbles were just loud enough to put a devious smirk on my face without being intrusive or overbearing.

My test car was fully loaded, including the $1,200 S Sport air suspension that also gets you racy red-painted brake calipers, and the setup was paired with the $2,500 23-inch wheels that are shroud in 285/35 front and 315/30 rear Hankook Ventus evo SUV summer performance tires, which look downright sick but likely contributed to the stiffer ride.

The S Sport air suspension felt a bit chattery over broken pavement in Balanced mode, and that slight chatter was amplified in Dynamic mode. Over a few particularly bumpy spots, Dynamic mode's suspension tuning made the SQ7 feel like a pebble skipping across the surface of a lake, so I kept it in Balanced mode, which was already firm enough. The steering wasn't overly communicative, but it was exceedingly direct without feeling frantic or darty, and I was still able to ascertain when the front tires were beginning to lose their fight with the laws of physics.

Despite the overly stiff suspension, I was utterly floored by the way the entire package came together to make a 5,500-pound SUV handle like it was on rails, a journalist cliché that is apt here. My co-driver was a self-proclaimed "coaster head," and he said the SQ7 really felt like a roller coaster when I was driving very sensibly down a tantalizingly rolling and winding road.

It also elicited a similar response when we found space to put Audi's 3.7-second 0-to-60 claim to the test and do a few launches. It's a good idea to secure any loose items in the vehicle before you activate launch control, because they'll rapidly become high-speed projectiles as the SQ7 shoves its occupants into the quilted leather thrones with dizzying force until the driver chickens out and lifts their right foot. It launches with the ferocity of an EV, but with all the good old fashioned drama that comes with several thousands of precisely timed explosions per minute and an occasional muscular kick from a gear change. It rocked — and it sucked gas. The trip odometer showed a dismal 12.5 mpg after my three-hour drive that mixed highway and a fair amount of admittedly intense backroad blasting, so it's no gas saver.

I'm sure you're tired of hearing me talk about how great the new Q7 and SQ7 are by now, and though they are both truly top tier vehicles, there are some little faults. The Q7 is still a sort of 'tweener model that straddles the midsize and full size classes; Audi reps described it as a do-it-all model that offers a small family the option to occasionally tote around three rows worth of children, but not it's necessarily meant for full-time three-row duties. I'd concur with that assessment, since it was difficult to squeeze past the second-row captain's chairs to climb into the third row, and once I got back there I realized the seat bottoms sit virtually on the floor of the car, so I ended up in an upright fetal position. The Q7's third row is still best suited for smaller humans with youthful joints.

Both of the cars I drove were optioned with the second-row captain's chairs, which look fantastic and are respectably comfortable, but there is no way to option ventilated second-row seats, and the seats did not recline far enough for true long-distance comfort. I sat in the second row of a Q7 for the two-hour drives from the airport to the hotel and vice versa, and I kept checking to make sure the seat backs didn't recline any further because I could not believe how limited their recline was. Second-row legroom was reasonable, but if the front seats were scooted back too far, it impinged upon the second-row space more than one may expect from such a large vehicle. I'm 6-foot-8 and mostly legs, so I'm definitely an outlier, but I try to think about more reasonable body shapes and sizes as well, and I still think the Q7's second row isn't as spacious as it could be.

It must be said that things looked and felt fantastic inside the Q7 and SQ7, with leather covering the entire door cards down to the generously sized door bins that easily accommodated my oversized Emotional Support Water Bottle. The use of black plastic trim has been kept to a minimum, aside from the giant bezels that surround the strangely shaped touchscreen arrangement, and I'm happy to report that the Q7's interior felt as solid, high-quality, and luxurious as an Audi ought to. The same goes for the materials in the second row, which isn't as common, and even the Q7's rear doors bring the leather all the way down to the door bins.

The Q7 uses Audi's current array of screens, and unfortunately that means there are no physical climate controls and not even any physical air vent adjustments — everything has to be done through the touchscreen. This shouldn't pose too significant of an issue for owners, since they will likely set their preferences and save them to their driver profile or the seat memory settings, but I'll never prefer adjusting my air vents through a touchscreen over a simple physical toggle.

The standard passenger screen is gimmicky to me, and anyone who read my Q9 or Mercedes-Benz GLE review will know that I hate when the passenger screen doesn't include passenger-specific controls. For example, the sport seats in my test cars all had adjustable side bolsters and thigh support, but the only way to adjust those functions was through the center infotainment screen. The passenger screen did incorporate controls for the passenger's massage settings, but forcing the passenger to reach across the dashboard and commandeer the entire center infotainment screen just to make a simple seat adjustment was infuriating. Plus, the infotainment screen is rather dramatically aimed at the driver, so it can be difficult to see and navigate from the passenger seat.

Beyond those gripes, though, the Q7 and SQ7 are exceptionally good midsize three-row luxury SUVs. In fact, I think they might be the most compelling cars in their segment, though I have yet to experience the new BMW X5. Some of Audi's recently revised products haven't exactly embodied the chic, stylish, and high-quality attributes that Audi's greatest models once did, but the Q7 and SQ7 are a return to form. They're stylish, well-priced, luxurious, solid-feeling, and exhilarating to drive, so I have to give it to the Ingolstadt team — they've outdone themselves on the new Q7.

#5559. An Insurer Paid Me $6.73 to Save a Teen's Life

— The No Surprises Act allows me to keep saving others

In October 2023, a 19-year-old came into the emergency department with a perforated appendix. By the time I got him to the operating room, his abdomen was full of pus. A surgery that normally takes me 15 minutes took an hour and a half due to the amount of inflammation distorting his anatomy. I rounded on him in the hospital every day for a week until he was stable for discharge, then followed him for another 2 months until he had fully recovered.

For that surgery, the week of inpatient care, and 2 months of post-op follow-up, the insurance company sent me a check for $6.73.

This wasn't a clerical error -- it was the contracted rate the insurance company had set for the procedure. Laparoscopic appendectomy, like most operations, falls under a 90-day global period, which meant I couldn't bill separately for any care delivered in the 90 days after surgery. As far as the insurer was concerned, $6.73 was full and final payment for treating a teenager with sepsis from a ruptured appendix.

This is precisely this kind of payment that makes the federal No Surprises Act's (NSA) independent arbitration process so important to physicians like me. The law gives us a way to challenge inadequate insurer payments through a neutral process, helping independent physicians keep our practices open so we can continue caring for patients and saving lives.

I opened my own practice in 2023 after several years in academic medicine, where I had learned exactly how little say a physician can have over her own patients, her own schedule, and her own worth. I wanted to make medical decisions based on what my patients needed, not on what a hospital system's spreadsheet required. I did not expect that the fight for that independence would move from the hospital administration to the insurance companies.

When I first opened my doors, I kept a part-time position at a county hospital to make ends meet while I built a patient base. When that wasn't enough, I added emergency general surgery call at another hospital for $40 an hour. For about 6 months I was working four jobs. My savings dwindled. I started drawing from my 401k. As a single mother and the sole provider for my two daughters, I was doing the math every month to make sure I could pay my staff, office rent, medical malpractice insurance, and my mortgage. For 2 years I did not pay myself, and I wondered whether staying in medicine was still something I could afford to do.

Lately, I find myself wondering whether I can keep practicing independently, because that $6.73 check was not an outlier; it is part of the pattern. A few months after the teen with the perforated appendix, another patient came in with severe anemia and a bowel obstruction due to a large colon tumor. I performed a robotic colectomy to remove the cancer, relieve the blockage, and stop the bleeding. The insurer denied the claim, stating the operation was "not medically necessary."

This is where the NSA arbitration process became critical to my practice. When an insurer and a physician can't agree on payment, each side submits an offer to a neutral arbitrator, who picks one. It isn't a settlement. It's one of the only checks that exists on an insurer's ability to set whatever price it wants for care the patient has already received.

Physicians win these disputes more than 80% of the time, and some critics have pointed to that number as evidence the system is broken. It isn't. It's evidence of how low insurers' offers actually are. An analysis of nearly 160,000 arbitration cases found insurers offered $0 to the physician in more than 20,000 cases. In the fourth quarter of 2024, roughly a quarter of all disputes were resolved by default, seemingly because the insurance company provided incomplete information or failed to make an offer at all. When one side offers nothing, or next to nothing, an arbitrator does not need to search hard for the fair number.

I did not go into medicine and open a solo practice in a saturated Houston market because I thought I could get rich. Sixteen years of education and training to be paid $6.73 for emergency surgery is a terrible way to build wealth, it turns out. I built my practice so that I could provide accessible surgical care while maintaining autonomy and agency over my life and professional choices. Every solo and small-practice physician I know who now uses this process was doing the same math I was doing at my kitchen table over the last 3 years, wondering if it was truly possible to practice medicine independently, outside of large institutions and private-equity-owned medical practices.

Arbitration is one of the only tools we have that puts a solo surgeon and a multibillion-dollar insurer on anything resembling equal footing.

The numbers show what happens when that tool disappears or is weakened by suggestions that doctors are the ones driving up costs. The share of physicians in independent practice fell from 37.8% in 2019 to 22.4% in 2024, with more than 75% of physicians now employed by hospitals or corporate entities. Every one of those doctors who left independent practice took something with them: continuity with patients they'd known for years and the ability to make a clinical decision without checking it against a corporate metric.

No physician is asking for sympathy. We are asking that the next time someone suggests that physicians are "gaming" the NSA, she instead asks why an insurer's opening offer for a week of care after a ruptured appendix was $6.73, and why it took a federal arbitrator and an 8-month process to make that number make sense.

The law was written to protect patients from surprise bills. What's often left out is that it also protects physicians who are trying to practice independently, because we are not interested in 5-minute visits and being told to double-book patients. Take this protection away and let end-stage corporatization and consolidation of healthcare finish the job, and we will witness the end of the independent practice of medicine in America.

#5558. LDV eDeliver 5 — A Chinese Bargain?

A bargain electric van from China — the LDV eDeliver 5. “The tide is changing; people are more on board with Chinese cars and electric vehicles” in Australia. And it has 50% more cargo space than the Korean competition (6.6 cubic metres). For the same price!

On the same day that we looked at the Kia PV5, we were also able to check out the LDV eDeliver 5. Chatting to the sales rep (Daniel), we discovered they had sold and delivered the one they had on the lot. Nevertheless, he told us some great stories and we caught up with the van at another dealership down the road later in the week.

The second dealership also sold Geely. The lot was full of them, and the rep told us several times how fast the Geely EX2 and EX5 were selling. Thank you, President Trump, and Supreme Leader Khamenei. Sales of Geely in Australia have doubled since the war started!

Anyway, back to the LDV eDeliver 5. For the curious, LDV stands for Leyland DAF Vans. After the collapse of British Leyland, the brand was sold to SAIC (along with MG). Since the eDeliver 5 launch in July, 77 units have sold in Australia. I felt that the number should be higher, agreeing with the comment found under the PV5 article: “High mileage work vehicles are the first frontier for EVs, not the last. That’s where there is the most financial benefit.” Sadly, though, commercial vans which work so hard around the suburbs, especially near Christmas, have been the slowest segment to electrify in Australia.

Another commentator made the valid point: “Business and fleet vehicles have consistent, predictable daily mileage, high daily mileage, thus huge annual battery utilization (therefore huge fuel savings), they don’t burn a ton of expensive fuel, and are purchased by financially rational decision makers.”

So, why aren’t more electric vans being bought in Australia? Running costs are lower, they are more comfortable, and the sticker price is now below price parity. Daniel and I had a fruitful discussion and came to the conclusion that the main thing stopping electric vans from being taken up in large numbers by fleet operators was the issue of the “fuel card.” It is complicated on the road because of multiple apps and providers, and even more if the van driver charges at home. How do we reimburse for home charging?

Surely this hurdle is not that hard to surmount — especially for contractors and owner operators. We’ll see as fuel insecurity in Australia continues. One owner reported back to Daniel that he has spent AU$3,000 to charge his eDeliver 5 over the past 50,000 km, compared with a diesel fuel bill of AU$14,000 for his old diesel van over the same distance. “Even if I sold the LDV van for nothing after 5 years, I would still be ahead.”

Apparently, some couriers are doing 500 km a day around the Brisbane suburbs. With a range of 320 km from its LFP batteries, the eDeliver 5 would have to top up during the day. Near our home in the northern suburbs of Brisbane, some service stations have installed high-speed chargers (BP Pulse is one example). I very rarely see anyone charging there. Perhaps that’s the charging that will be used by electric delivery vans. LDV also makes larger models, the eDeliver 7 and 9.

Daniel usually only sells two vans a year. He tells me that he has sold five eDeliver 5’s in the past 6 weeks! August to November is their peak time for sales. The tide is indeed changing. The diesel Deliver 5 is AU$10,000–12,000 cheaper than the electric, but with the large kilometres they drive it would not be long before this difference was covered by fuel savings. The eDeliver has V2L capability, putting out 6.6 kWh charge for added convenience. One local customer runs a fridge in the back of the van from the V2L capability.

As an example of the effect of the Iran/USA war, Daniel told us of a customer who bought a new diesel ute 3 weeks before the war started and then came back and wanted to trade it in. He even offered his wife’s car as part of the deal. But the numbers just wouldn’t add up.

Service costs roughly $735 every two years or 30,000 km on the eDeliver 5. Compare this with the Toyota HiAce. The diesel needs a service every 10,000 km. Daniel tells me that during EV service, they check the oil in the gearbox and the coolant for the battery. “Couriers like the power on/off button. A lot of people are screen scared.” The eDeliver 5 has a fob, it is a cheaper option than connectivity.

Of course, a Facebook page has been started. I joined it seeking more real-life experiences.

“I have one that I’ve been driving for a month now. I use it for work and have tools in the back, so the weight of the car is 2.4 tonnes. With driving around town (mostly city and some highway). I get around 250 km before I have to charge again from about 10%.

“It’s a great ride and very comfy. I do maybe 100 km per day, so charge every second or third. Works great for me.”

“There has been a seismic shift in consumer behaviour due to recent geo-political tensions, fuel insecurity, and surging diesel costs,” said Dinesh Chinnappa, the general manager of LDV Australia. “With that in mind we brought forward our plans to introduce the LDV eDeliver 5 to the Australian market, which is why stock will initially be limited.”

You can check out the specs here. Of course it has all the safety features you would expect. For those who like to watch, check out the video review of the eDeliver 7.

There are several small cargo vans now available on the Australian market. I will endeavour to check out a few more and report back to CleanTechnica readers. As we hurtle towards Christmas and the online orders increase, I expect to see many more electric Santas delivering parcels to front doors in the suburbs of Brisbane.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5557. At $22,000, Is This 2006 BMW E46 M3 The Gold Standard?

With every generation, BMW has made the M3 (and later M4) better. Today's Nice Price or No Dice E46 might be the baby bear "just right" edition, since it's "not too slow" and "not too complicated." Let's see if its price tag proves likewise.

It is, of course, perfectly okay to be average. That is, after all, what most of us in the world are. It's a statistical reality required to define outliers to the norm. Or something like that. It's been a long time since I took either algebra or stats.

At first glance, the 2002 Honda CR-V EX AWD we looked at last Friday seemed pretty average. CR-Vs are ubiquitous on the road, and our car wasn't growing a third arm off its roof or practicing freaky Looksmaxing in any way. What made the Honda above average in interest came down to two factors. First, it had a five-speed manual gearbox, a rarity on the model. The second was that while it had crazy high (241K) mileage, its owners had cared enough to keep it on the road to the point of going to the expense of fitting it with a lower-mileage engine. At $4,000, it was also priced right in the middle: not suspiciously low to trigger spidey senses that it might be a scam, nor so high as to make interested parties think the seller lives in an alternate reality. Ultimately, that resolved into a 77% 'Nice Price' win.

Few car companies exemplify the idea of overachievement better than BMW. Rising from borrowed microcar obscurity out of the ashes of WWII, the Bavarian brand built a reputation for sporting saloons and coupes. So successful was the effort that by the early 1970s the company's cars were considered benchmarks, with capable competitors like Datsun's 510 measured against the marque and described as "the poor man's BMW."

No model better exemplifies BMW's achievements as a marque than the M3. Along with the M5, the 3 Series-based sports car introduced the company's in-house motorsports division and a badge that quickly became inspirational to many enthusiasts.

The 2006 BMW M3 coupe we're looking at today represents what is arguably the pinnacle of M3-ness. No, it's not the most capable, highest-powered, or most technologically advanced in the line. What it is, though, is one of the last of the almost purely analog cars, and this one is kitted for the kind of driver that seeks out slinky bends, not slothful boulevards.

First off, this M3 rolls double sixes. That's the 333-horsepower 3.2-liter S54 straight-six under the hood and the Getrag 420G six-speed stick that backs it up. Together, they have powered this M3 to a mere 55,732 miles, according to the ad. That works out to just under 2,800 miles a year.

Confusingly, the ad runs under the "For Sale by Owner" section of Craigslist, although the pictures imply that it's dealer-offered. That, or the current owner lives in a commercial-style space with an NA Miata parked in the living room.

Regardless, the ad doesn't give us much to go on regarding the car's condition, maintenance history, or whether it's spent its entire existence doing donuts in parking lots. All we get instead is the descriptor that the car "Runs great." As Craigslist listings require, we are also told the car has a clean title, so there's that. A PPI by a qualified BMW wrench-turner would be recommended for any interested party.

The pictures do tell a story, though. It shows the car to be painted in Phoenix Yellow, a fairly rare color and one that stands out from the crowd. Underneath the expressive hue, the bodywork appears to be in perfect condition, with no obvious dings, scrapes, or missing pieces. One odd choice appears to be the capping of the side marker lights on the front bumper, though that seems to be something M3 owners like to do, if the Internet is any indication. Underneath all that, the car wears Style 163M Cross Spoke alloys. Again, no word is given on the age or condition of the tires wrapping those.

The cabin is remarkable for being so unremarkable. Some natural creasing and buffing is evident on the driver's seat side bolster, but that's the cost of doing business in any car with cosseting seats. Nicely, however, there's no evidence of greasy mitts on the Alcantara steering wheel or leather shift knob. Everything is gloriously stock in here, right down to BMW's somewhat cryptic stereo and climate controls. Someone has also done an excellent job of vacuum-striping the floor mats, which is very zen and satisfying to see.

As noted, the seller is frustratingly brief in the ad's description. Oddly, they do say that prospective tire-kickers "Must provide own transportation." Do they imagine people expect to be picked up at the airport and brought to see the M3, like the seller is running a livery service as a side hustle? Who knows. Besides, we need to focus on this BMW's $22,000 asking price. Yes, that amount will get you a slightly used Kia with some warranty left; what's your point?

What do you say, is this clean and capable E46 M3 worth that $22,000 asking despite the info-light ad description and the requirement of arranging your own transportation to simply view it? Or is that too much to ask for an ad that says so little?

Boston, Massachusetts, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

Help me out with Nice Price or No Dice. Hit me up at robemslie@gmail.com and send a fixed-price tip. Remember to include your commenter handle.

#5556. Metformin and Long COVID; Lp(a) Lowering for Cardiovascular Disease

— Also in TTHealthWatch: retatrutide for knee osteoarthritis

TTHealthWatch is a weekly podcast from Texas Tech. In it, Elizabeth Tracey, director of electronic media for Johns Hopkins Medicine in Baltimore, and Rick Lange, MD, president of Texas Tech Health El Paso, look at the top medical stories of the week.

This week's topics include intensive blood pressure lowering in people with diabetes; metformin for long COVID; lowering lipoprotein(a), or Lp(a), and cardiovascular events; and a triple agonist for knee osteoarthritis.

0:50 Lowering Lp(a) and cardiovascular disease

1:50 Pelacarsen administered subcutaneously

2:50 More risk factors you reduce the better

3:15 Retatrutide for knee osteoarthritis and obesity

4:15 Did reduce knee pain as well as reduced weight

5:15 Almost 38% reduction in placebo group

6:00 Intensive blood pressure lowering in type 2 diabetes

7:00 Doesn't reduce major cardiovascular events

9:50 Looked like a benefit with metformin

Elizabeth: Can metformin prevent the development of long COVID?

Rick: Does lowering Lp-little-a levels prevent cardiovascular events?

Elizabeth: A GLP-1-plus agonist for knee osteoarthritis in patients who are overweight.

Rick: And is there a benefit to intensive blood pressure lowering in people with type 2 diabetes?

Elizabeth: That's what we're talking about this week on TTHealthWatch, your weekly look at the medical headlines from Texas Tech University Health Sciences Center in El Paso. I'm Elizabeth Tracey, a Baltimore-based medical journalist.

Rick: And I'm Rick Lange, president of Texas Tech Health El Paso.

Elizabeth: Well, Rick, because the AHA [American Heart Association] meeting has been ongoing, we're talking an awful lot about cardiovascular disease this week. And so why don't we turn directly to the New England Journal of Medicine? And I'm going to toss the ball to you. Which of those two you served up would you like to talk about first?

Rick: Well, let's turn to the one that I think is most surprising. And that is the question, does lowering Lp(a) levels reduce cardiovascular events? Now, for people that aren't familiar, Lp-little-a, or what's called lipoprotein(a), is known to be a risk factor for atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease. And on many different levels, it's been shown to be associated with atherosclerosis. For a long time, we've not had any really good way of lowering it. Until recently, we have a number of new medications that have been shown to very effectively lower it, as much as 75% to 90%. And the presumption was, if you did that, you'd lower the risk of cardiovascular events.

But that really wasn't tested until this study just came out in the New England Journal of Medicine. Pelacarsen, it's an antisense oligonucleotide, which is very effective at lowering Lp(a) levels. And they administered it to about 4,100 patients subcutaneously on a monthly basis. And they followed these individuals over the course of 4.7 years.

Those that received pelacarsen had a significant decrease in their lipoprotein(a), over 70%. They looked at death, heart attack, stroke, or someone needing coronary revascularization. There was no difference at all in those that received pelacarsen and those that received placebo. What about those that had a baseline Lp(a) that was a little bit higher? Even in that group, there was no benefit with regard to decreasing cardiovascular events.

Elizabeth: I think a lot of the analysis of this trial are just really fascinating for people speculating on why in the world didn't this work. Is this just a stand-in for something else? And the other thing is, even in people who have that genetic predisposition to elevated Lp(a) levels, these folks were already on other things to try to keep them at lower risk. And so maybe the delta just wasn't high enough to show up.

Rick: We know that when you reduce cardiovascular risk factors, the more that you reduce, the better outcome you get. And so it was hypothesized that, sure, we could get your cholesterol down, but lowering Lp(a) would even be more advantageous. But apparently, it doesn't appear to be so. There are other agents that are being tested. It'll be interesting to see whether they have any benefit.

Elizabeth: More to come. Let's turn then to retatrutide once weekly in adults with knee osteoarthritis pain and obesity.

Retatrutide is an investigational triple agonist of GIP, GLP-1, and glucagon receptors. Sort of this idea that if we take a multifactorial approach to all of these things, is that going to result in a better outcome? They enrolled adults without diabetes who had painful knee osteoarthritis and a BMI [body mass index] of at least 27. They were randomly assigned to receive once-weekly subcutaneous injections at a couple dosages of this med or placebo. And they were looking at the change in their pain score as far as their knee osteoarthritis was concerned, and the percentage change in body weight at week 68. They had 445 participants in one arm of the trial, 574 in another.

And what they showed was that, sure enough, the retatrutide was able to have a significant impact on this knee osteoarthritis score. And clearly, they also had percentage changes in body weight that were significant as well. Sure enough, if you use this triple agonist in patients who come in with painful knee osteoarthritis and obesity, you reduce both their pain score and you reduce their obesity. For me, this trial really harkens to that same question of which is it, chicken or egg? Is it that you unweight the knee and therefore you result in less painful symptomatology?

Rick: Yes. This particular agent lowered weight and reduced knee pain. You're not terribly surprised because anything you do that reduces weight reduces knee pain. You could argue that it's that effect alone and nothing else. Although when you decrease fat cells, you decrease inflammation. I'd be interested in knowing if you took people that weren't obese and gave them this agent, would it reduce knee pain? Is there anything in here that surprised you at all?

Elizabeth: In the retatrutide group, they had a reduction of at least 50% in their pain score, but that was compared with 37%, almost 38%, reduction in those in the placebo group. And we've noted this in the past.

Rick: Yeah. Placebo effect. That's why all studies to assess whether a treatment is effective has to be compared to some placebo.

Elizabeth: Right. And of course, we have to note that we see the same side effects that we see with almost everything that's relative to the GLP-1s: nausea, diarrhea, constipation. For the most part, they resolved over time.

Rick: And they're usually associated with dose escalation. For people that are taking a GLP-1, I usually advise them, "Listen, as we increase the dose, we're going to do it slowly and methodically. You may experience these." But as you mentioned, it's usually mild and it will go away if they'll persist with it.

Elizabeth: OK. Still staying in the New England Journal.

Rick: This is the third study that I'm aware of that looks to see whether intensive blood pressure lowering is beneficial in people with type 2 diabetes. It follows on two other studies. One was the ACCORD study, and it showed that intensive blood pressure lowering, trying to get it below 120, was not beneficial. And they followed that with a study in China that did the same thing. And it showed just the opposite effect, primarily by reducing strokes, which are more prevalent in the Chinese population.

This study was done in Brazil, about 9,400 individuals, ethnically diverse. About two-thirds were women that had hypertension, type 2 diabetes, and a high cardiovascular risk. And they randomized them to blood pressure lowering to either get it below 140 versus 120, and followed these individuals over a mean follow-up of 4 years.

There was really no significant difference. So in this ethnically-diverse population, it appears that intensive blood pressure lowering in people with type 2 diabetes really doesn't reduce the occurrence of major cardiovascular events.

Elizabeth: Of course, what we know is that when we attempt these intensive blood pressure-lowering regimens, people object to polypharmacy, and particularly those who already have diabetes and are already taking other meds.

Rick: Yeah. In fact, for the individuals that were taking the less intensive, the median of 2.3 different medications, and those on the more intensive, 3.2. This was so well done. Oftentimes, when you get really intensive therapy, you have more episodes of hypotension and syncope falling out, sometimes fractures. But in this particular study, not. They approached it very methodically. It's just that it just didn't really help.

Elizabeth: Let's turn to the Journal of Medical Virology. I think this might be the first time we've ever actually talked about a study that was in this. They were looking at metformin at the time of COVID-19 infection and the risk of long COVID. And this, they identify as a target trial emulation study. They were looking to evaluate the impact of metformin prescribed at the time of COVID infection on the risk of developing long COVID, and they did it by examining electronic health records.

They conducted a new-user analysis of metformin, prescribed within 6 days of documented infection with COVID, versus experimental controls. And those were prescriptions for fluvoxamine, fluticasone, ivermectin, or montelukast. They had to have, as their inclusion criteria, a clinic visit in the 0 to 6 months and 6 to 12 months before infection. They excluded all folks who had had metformin or control within 12 months, and their primary outcome was long COVID development or death.

The number that they actually came up with was 248 in the metformin and control groups with an average age of 53. They had 4% in the metformin group develop long COVID or death versus 8.5% in the control group, seeming to show that there's a benefit of prescribing metformin when somebody presents with a new COVID infection.

Rick: As you mentioned, this was a different kind of study. It's an emulation trial. Usually, we look at randomized controlled trials as appearing to be the gold standard.

So in this particular case, individuals with COVID, some of whom received metformin, and then the other group, let's give them some medication that is not known to be beneficial with regard to COVID, because there are some in vitro and in vivo studies that suggest there is a benefit of metformin in people that have COVID. So those are the active comparators. They used an antidepressant, an anti-inflammatory, an anti-parasite, and then a respiratory drug. It looked like there was a benefit, a reduction in long COVID symptoms attributed to the metformin, because they didn't see with any of these other medications. So that's how this emulation study was done.

Elizabeth: Based on this study, Rick, would you say that you would, especially as we see different variants emerging, if one emerges that's going to be more severe, would you use metformin in patients with a new diagnosis of COVID?

Rick: That's an important distinction. So first of all, it's a new diagnosis. So they were given this within 6 days of the diagnosis. I would be inclined to offer it to the patient and say, "We're not going to be on this long term, but there is some data that suggests it can reduce the risk." I'll let the patient be involved, but I would not have any qualms about it. There's no harm associated in any of the studies. And again, there's pretty convincing data that it reduces the risk of long COVID symptoms by about 50%. So the answer is yes. Elizabeth, let me take it back to you. Would you take it if I prescribed it?

Elizabeth: Oh, I would take it for sure. I would also note that they used a phrase in this paper that I was completely unfamiliar with. They say, "An experimental control reduces unmeasured confounding, such as the likelihood of engagement with healthcare, immortal time bias, and the placebo effect." I've never heard of an "immortal time bias" before.

Rick: That has to do with when the diagnosis is made, when things are given. I'll give you a great example. If we're looking at how long people live and you have a patient group that's 5 years old, they're likely to live for another 80 years. If you start with people that are in their 80s, they're likely to live 5 years. Is there a mortality difference? It has to do with the time that the patient is enrolled in the trial, not the long-term outcome.

Elizabeth: Thank you for that. And on that note then, that's a look at this week's medical headlines from Texas Tech. I'm Elizabeth Tracey.

Rick: And I'm Rick Lange. Y'all listen up and make healthy choices.

#5555. 2028 Mercedes-Benz GLA EV Is A Semi-Charmed Kind Of SUV

Driving a 2028 Mercedes-Benz GLA won't make your nostalgic radio station jam session sound sweeter, though the 16-speaker Burmester surround sound system is very good. Collective Soul, your friend of nearly 14 years and yourself won't harmonize any better. What the third-generation GLA will do, however, is provide a frictionless, airy and easy vessel for making memories in the sun. Turn up the volume using the haptic volume knob, turn on your fixed glass panoramic roof, and heaven, let your light shine down.

Mercedes had a few journalists out to Spain to drive the 2028 GLA250+ and GLA350 in their pure EV forms. Taking a ride up the Spanish coast in a car with a glass roof is a pretty fantastic way to spend a day. I only wished I was driving a convertible along the brilliant Spanish coast once (OK, maybe twice) during the whole drive thanks to a wide open airy cabin and whisper-quiet interior. This is a workhorse of an EV, not a pleasure cruiser, and when we got down to brass tacks it was clear there is still some work to be done.

Full disclosure: Mercedes-Benz paid for me to fly to Spain, set me loose on the Spanish countryside in two fully charged GLAs, kept me fed and put me up in a hotel for two nights.

This is Mercedes-Benz's subcompact SUV, though this third-gen GLA is anything but sub or compact, having grown in every direction and stretching what it means to be a part of this category. The outgoing GLA has been on the roster since 2019, and Mercedes is hoping to give the GLA a shot in the arm with this new, more sporty design. It can be had with either hybrid or fully electric powertrains, the latter coming in single-motor, rear-wheel-drive GLA250+ and dual-motor, all-wheel drive GLA350 forms, mirroring the CLA and GLB it shares its platform with. Both have an 85-kWh nickel-manganese-cobalt battery pack that can be DC fast-charged at up to 320 kW, taking it from 10% to 80% in just 22 minutes, and a DC converter means it can be charged at both 800-volt and 400-volt stations. Drivers can adjust the regenerative braking via paddles on the back of the steering wheel. The settings range from hardly any at all, to a gentle tug, to an abrupt, lurching bite at its highest setting that I would think would be impossible to live with unless you were a real weirdo about range-maxxing.

The GLA250+ was the first tester I took out along the Spanish coast, and it was a genuine hoot. The winding roads were sharp and unfamiliar, but the GLA was sure-footed, whipping into the curves easily and handily. Normally, an EV drive on curvy roads would send my sensitive stomach into upheaval, but not in the GLA. My gut is rarely wrong about such things. The motor on its rear axle makes 268 horsepower and 247 pound-feet of torque, and it offers a range of up to 408 miles on the European WLTP cycle, which should translate to the mid-300s when the EPA gets its hands on it. The GLA250 is an everything car, from parking easily in the city to doing long road trips in serious comfort and commuting on the freeways with that nice EV torque surge for handy merging.

That was not the case with the GLA350. Its pair of motors put out 349 hp and 380 lb-ft, enough to hit 60 mph from a standstill in 5.3 seconds (almost two seconds quicker than the GLA250), and the car just doesn't need the extra punch of power. What had been a relaxing drive up the stunning Spanish coast in the GLA250+ was punctuated by over-torqued jumps from dead stop. It just didn't make for a good driving experience. One motor and rear-wheel drive is really all you need. The range loss for the 4Matic model makes it extra not worth it, but the driving dynamics are enough to make me opt for the less powerful version.

The hybrid will come to our shores late next year after the EV, with a turbocharged 1.5-liter four-cylinder engine mated to a 48-volt lithium-ion battery and a small electric motor. When driving conditions require less than 30 hp, the electric motor takes over most of the momentum, adding its extra oomph to the gas engine at higher speeds.

One thing Mercedes-Benz made clear to us journalists is that the new GLA is big. Every one of the GLA's measurements has grown compared to the outgoing model, including the wheelbase that has been stretched by 2.4 inches to 109.8 inches. It's six inches longer overall, too. At this rate, the next generation of GLA will be merely a compact SUV.

The front seats are comfortable and cushy, but the heating and massage elements aren't much to write a blog about. I might be a bad judge here, though, because I need to cook like a two-day-old 7/11 taquito in my car seat. The massage with heat option felt less like relaxing and more like I accidentally sat on someone with a bad fever and they were weakly poking me to alert me of my mistake. Unsettled, I turned it off.

Rear passengers get a whole inch and a half of extra legroom, and max headroom for front seat passengers grew almost an inch. Do these measurements seem like things you normally worry about in a car? Maybe not, but the overall effect works. The space in the back made me think this SUV would be great for parents of older kids, or childless folks who want to bring friends on trips but don't want to make them suffer a stuffy back seat. The back didn't strike me as particularly car-seat ready, though, and while the new GLA does have a small frunk, the general storage situation would be tough with most younger kids. My prediction: the GLA won't baby all that well.

Is it a bit blob shaped? Yes. Is that the general shape of a lot of SUVs now for incredibly important, crash-worthy, life-saving reasons? Also yes. I'm down with the blob, just give us some cool colors. Sadly, Mercedes does not give us many cool colors. There's red, blue, five shades of gray, and a new greenish gray called Liquid Crystal Green.

Of course, it wouldn't be a new car without a blackout package, with Mercedes' Night package being offered alongside a matte black paint job. The GLA is also available with the AMG Line appearance package, which adds inches to your wheels and sporty elements to the interior, like stainless steel sport pedals and AMG sport seats. The AMG Line also comes with bright red seatbelts, which adds a much-needed pop of color to the interior, though sometimes it felt like a teacher trying to make safety fun for the class. 18-inch wheels are standard, but they can be bumped up to 19s or 20s.

Mercedes-Benz says it is aiming for a "welcome home" feel when folks enter their cars. There's a real lack of rescue pit bull nose prints on the glass for it to feel homey to me, but maybe for a classier breed of person who owns a classier breed of dog that rings true. The smoked-glass-effect Iconic grille on this beefy SUV does remind me of the nose of an exuberant puppy. Thanks to the additional height on the GLA, the new grille doesn't overwhelm the front fascia to the same degree as on the C-Class EV. The lights embedded inside the glass grille give it a more jeweled appearance during the day than the black empty space of the last ICE generation. At night, 608 backlit polycarbonate lights turn the grille into a stunning display. A bunch of little lights always recalls celebrations like Christmas or bonfire nights, so the "Welcome Home" effect Mercedes is looking for snagged me there.

The MBUX AI Assistant still sucks. It's still a pain to turn off, it's still listening constantly, and it's still interrupting conversations unprompted or at the mere hint of its makers' name, something bandied about quite a lot by two journalists driving its car. When called upon to do the parlor trick of finding a specific alternative rock song or find a restaurant with dietary needs, it was often flumoxxed or demanded a log-in to some kind of account. Asking indirectly for information on a song, however, I got an interesting Pop-Up Videos–style spiel about the song and the car then asked "Would you like me to play it?" A radio station based on that song followed afterwards.

Last time I faced down the AI Assistant in the C-Class, it questioned my grip on my emotions because I was singing fairly tame Mountain Goats tunes (seriously, it wasn't even a tune about lunar cannibalism). At least on this drive it didn't try to direct me towards a domestic violence shelter for singing along to "Better Man" by Pearl Jam. Mercedes says its AI Assistant is an amalgamation of the best bits of ChatGPT, Google and Microsoft's large language models. If this is the peak, color me unimpressed. The average non-tech person either thinks LLMs are a neat trick, or they loathe it entirely. Mercedes would do well to make this feature a little easier to switch off.

Gang, I may be suffering from screen fatigue. Between phones, laptops for work and car interiors, it feels like I'm always numbly poking at an unfeeling pane of glass. There's a metaphor for life in there somewhere. The GLA comes with the dash and infotainment-swallowing giant screens surrounded by easily smudged piano black bezel, and this layout is feeling less luxurious by the day. Luxury should have more heft to it, more of a solid haptic feedback than crisp displays on top of crisp displays. Screens have gone from "this car is like a spaceship" to "this car is just like every other car on the road." That just ain't luxury, jack.

Case in point — I went to crank up the volume on the steering wheel to properly honor a Third Eye Blind song, and was thrilled to find the old roller volume knob right where I needed it on the steering wheel, but the control felt cheap and plasticky. Ditto on the weird whale tail–shaped window switches. Still, I'd take plastic bobbles for stuff like the HVAC and seat heating over navigating through touchscreen menu after touchscreen menu or turning to my long-time nemesis, the MBUX AI assistant. Turning off the AI, for instance, became a flurry of navigating touchscreens until my drive partner and I gave up after several attempts, and if there's one thing we journos know, it's how to mess with the settings. On either side of the screen are Mercedes' circular climate control air vents, which are also showing their age. Every time I see them I make the "Virtual Insanity" music video joke. Both the joke and design are well past their prime.

Then, there's the much-lauded passenger screen. I realize I sound like a cranky old woman complaining about luxury automakers these days with their screens and AI Assistants and corny interior lighting accents (yeah, I dislike those too), and maybe there are some folks out there who like to just ride along up front with the driver in their own little worlds, but tuning out to the point you're watching a whole show on your own 14-inch infotainment screen? Feels a bit rude and isolating, like having a loud phone conversation while trapped in an elevator with someone. This isn't a car to be chauffeured in, it's for families and friends and people who supposedly like each other. If you're on a long trip and need to tune out for a bit, that's what we have phones for.

The entire effect is a solid car with a lot of nonsense that makes it seem like Mercedes might be chasing trends rather than leading by example in the luxury market. Those blank, black bezeled areas between screens are a bleak reminder of where the thoughtful luxury used to go. Maybe I'm being too critical, but as an entry-level vehicle you have to hope it will set up buyers for a lifetime of returning to Mercedes-Benz and I am not sure seizing on the latest technological fads moves that goal post any closer for the brand.

#5554. Elon Musk Interview At The DealBook Summit: Starlink Is Revolutionary And Saves Lives

Elon Musk, the chief executive of SpaceX and Tesla, said at the DealBook Summit recently that “the number of lives that have been saved” by Starlink connectivity “may be hundreds.”

Want to get the correct numbers? Musk asked host Andrew Ross Sorkin, editor of DealBook, a daily business and policy report from The New York Times. “It’s all over the internet. Just do a search.”

It was an ill-placed flippant comment, considering that Ukraine has suffered repeated disruptions to their access to Starshield — the military version of SpaceX’s Starlink.

In an editorial following President Donald J. Trump’s meeting this week with Russian President Putin and Chinese President Xi, former Secretary of State Hillary Clinton reminds readers that, according to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, Ukrainian access to US early warning intelligence about incoming Russian missile strikes has been fractured, and Ukraine’s access to Starshield’s service remains limited.

Starshield helps Ukraine shoot down Russian attack drones. Limited access to Starshield means increased Ukrainian deaths as the country battles against Russian aggression — the opposite of Musk’s broad brush claim of lives saved by his technology.

Musk did address restrictions on the Ukrainian military’s access to the SpaceX satellite communications network during the Deal book Summit. He said that Starlink was never meant to be used for offensive strikes in war.

Historian and activist Rebecca Solnit refuses to dismiss Musk’s handling of Ukranian airspace as mere commerce. Elon Musk “has made much of his obscene pile of money off the US government with SpaceX and StarLink,” Solnit outlines. “They have given him power that should never belong to individuals – as demonstrated by his return to manipulating StarLink in ways that impair Ukrainian efforts in the war with Russia.”

Representative Jim Himes (D-CT), the top-ranking Democrat on the House Intelligence Committee, has written to Elon Musk with a formal request “that senior SpaceX officials meet with me to explain the Starshield service disruptions in Ukraine in October 2026.”

Himes notes that SpaceX, Musk’s company, is “a US Government purchased service established via a contract between the U.S. Government and SpaceX.” Himes wants to know “why those changes were made, and whether those changes were made unilaterally by SpaceX or were directed by a US Government official.” Since SpaceX has potentially modified a US Government contract, “combined with its lack of responsiveness to congressional oversight,” Himes argues that “every contract the US Government has with Space X” should be questioned.

Reducing the controversy to everyday terms, Musk insisted during the DealBook Summit that the Starlink design is “revolutionary,” in that it can be placed on a roof. “It solves the problem of reaching people who are in a building.” Sure, gaps in service continue, but Musk has a solution. “The missing piece of the puzzle” to making Starlink ubiquitous is an alternative to a metal roof, he suggested. The solution is “terrestrial… we can establish micro-ground stations… a network that are, maybe, bound together with Starlink terminals. Instead of big cell towers all over the place.”

Elon Musk is one of the richest people in the world, with significant political clout. He controls the direction and pace of one of the most important satellite technologies on which the US and the world depends, as former Secretary of Labor Robert Reich explains. Musk is one of the “authoritarians of the new economy,” Reich encapsulates, and Musk has “unparalleled power over every aspect of the lives of Americans and millions of others around the world.”

Reich adds, “The Democrats should investigate why Elon Musk funneled a half-billion dollars into the 2026 midterms. Was it because he wants to keep his satellite monopoly? His defense contracts?”

Musk has a lot on his mind these days. Sorkin reminded Musk that he had once said that his “mind was like a storm.”

Musk responded that, right now, “I feel relatively at peace.”

Carolyn Fortuna, PhD, is a longtime journalist and researcher for CleanTechnica who frequently writes about climate science, renewable energy, and environmental communication. Since joining the publication in 2016, Fortuna has authored nearly 1,900 articles focusing on ecojustice, clean technology adoption, and the intersection of science and public policy. #5553. These Are The Road Rage Moments Which Most Mirrored GTA 6 In Your Real Life

Some people don't need to fire up "Grand Theft Auto 6" on a gaming console to experience the terror of a chaotic populace. The universe Rockstar Games crafted over two decades is over-the-top, but it has always been a satirical reflection of American society. This reflection also includes how quickly some of our fellow drivers are willing to resort to violence over the smallest grievance. However, our country's healthcare system hasn't discovered respawning despite how much money we've poured into it.

We asked our readers last week which road rage moments from their real lives most mirrored what they would see in "Grand Theft Auto 6." The responses in the comment section didn't disappoint with how bizarre, hilarious and terrifying these incidents were. Beyond the traditional roadside fistfights, we had a snowball toted like a handgun, altercations with actual firearms and BMW drivers reinforcing their terrible reputation. Without further ado, here are the incidents that wouldn't look out of place in Vice City:

Back in HS, A friend of mine and I were driving around late night and while stopped at an intersection a guy in a Jeep took the corner really fast and almost sideswiped us. My friend gave him the middle finger out of the sunroof. Next thing we knew, we're in a high-speed chase involving the guy, who tried ramming us off the road multiple times. He caught us in a parking lot, blocked us from leaving and pointed a LARGE revolver at us. I grabbed my buddy's cell phone, held it up and screamed "I'm on the phone with the cops!" (even though I wasn't). That gave us enough time to drive over a curb, ripping his mufflers off, and drive up an embankment and high-tail it to the police station.

Moral of the story: Don't flip people off. Let it go and go on with your day.

A couple of years ago, I was walking through San Francisco's Embarcadero District on my way to Radwood NorCal (which was held on one of the piers that year). I overheard some yelling and turned my head to see a blue Jeep Cherokee XJ pulled over on the side of the road. The Jeep driver and a random homeless guy were in each other's face, yelling at each other. From what I made out, the driver accused the homeless guy of jaywalking right in front of him and spitting at his Jeep while the homeless guy argued that he was a pedestrian and that he had the right of way. Fortunately, it was just words being exchanged and no physical blows. The Jeep driver decided he had enough and took off with a loud squeal of tires.

It's so long ago that I don't remember what precipitated it, what happened, or how I behaved but here's what I do remember:

It was late afternoon. I was driving down 580 South through Berkeley, CA on my way to the San Francisco-Oakland Bay Bridge. And here's how it ended: the other driver was on my left, his passenger-side window was open, and suddenly there was a VERY LOUD BANG from something hitting the side of my car. I hit the brakes so he'd shoot past me. He sped off, I kept my distance.

Later, I found a strange dent by the rear pillar (1997? Mazda Navajo LX (aka Ford Explorer Sport), like a thumb had pressed in the metal, but no hole in the metal or even the slightest break in the paint.

I don't know, maybe he fired a .22 at me? A BB gun? Regardless, it was pretty frightening.

I was walking in downtown Chicago. A guy pulls to a stop at a red light at an intersection. He gets out of his car, nonchalantly walks up to a woman standing on the sidewalk waiting to cross the street, slaps her, then immediately walks calmly back to his car just as the light turns green, and drives away*. I assume they knew each other, because the woman did not react even a little, and continued just standing there with zero expression on her face.

*: He got instant karma, as there was a police cruiser 2 cars behind him, who then pulled him over on the next block.

About 15 years ago, my wife and I were sitting in her Chevy Cobalt in a parking spot in front of Hobby Lobby in Lincoln, NE during the holiday shopping season. It was snowing heavily (which will become important in a moment). We were on the phone with our cable company trying to figure out a billing error when another car pulls in next to us. The driver opens her door so hard it slams into our passenger door. I roll down the window and say something to the effect of, "Thanks for the door ding." Nothing demeaning. No name-calling. No F-you. Literally just sarcastically said what I said. Suddenly the husband comes flying around the car screaming at me, tries to open my door (which I quickly locked), and when he realizes he can't get in goes to the front of the car and begins making a snowball out of the fresh snow. He literally holds his arm as if he is going to throw it at me while his wife joins in with the yelling. The cable company rep on our speakerphone is asking us if we need to hang up to call 911. We are in our car dying of laughter at this fool who is going to use a snowball as a weapon.

As they settle down and start walking into the store, we end our phone conversation and decide to exit our vehicle, as their loud yelling drew a small crowd, so we knew we would be safe. As I open my door, the husband and wife come flying back out of the store yelling at us for door-dinging THEIR car (I hadn't) and the husband slips in the snow and eats it. Serves him right.

Was first in line waiting to turn left on a red light that had an advance green. I heard sirens, so when the light changed to green I stayed still.

The 5 Series BMW behind me was blaring his horn and making a verbal commotion. Then he decided to swing around me on the right side to make the left-hand turn.

He was so busy looking at me sitting there that he almost didn't notice the ambulance right in front of him. (with lights and sirens). He slammed his brakes to a very sudden stop and the ambulance had to swerve around him.

I saw him look in the rearview mirror at me. I smiled and waved.

This is more GTA San Andreas. I worked as a buyer in California for a large dealer that had blue uniform shirts (hint hint). My coworker was test-driving a customer car we were making an offer on, which was a bright blue Dodge Challenger with aftermarket matching blue rims.

He's sitting at a stoplight in his blue work shirt and bright blue car when a guy dressed completely in red, who was walking down the street, started yelling and running towards him. Luckily, the light turned green and my coworker gunned it before the guy got too close and things escalated further.

This was in the '90s. I was taking the Super Shuttle back to BWI from downtown DC and on the side of the road, a Mercedes and a BMW were pulled over and two dudes in suits were duking it out. Full-on fisticuffs in the breakdown lane, black eyes and bloody ties!

Stopped at a red light that had been red for about five seconds before I got to it. Dude in a black BMW 5 Series rolled up behind me and started blaring his horn. When that had no effect, he got out screaming with rage and started to approach my driver's door.

At which time, seeing no cross-traffic, I accelerated through the red and called 911. He got back in his car, raced through the red after me. Being about six blocks from the police station, I told the operator not to bother sending the police: I'd bring him to them.

He followed me right to the police station, tailgating me and blaring his horn the whole way as screaming out the window and giving me the middle finger. And boy was he in for a nasty surprise when we got there.

I love some cartoon violence. When I lived in Vancouver, a number of years ago, I'd just hopped off the bus downtown. A dude ran past me, reached out and snatched the purse off the shoulder of an older lady. He took off faster than I expected him to be able to run. If he'd just kept going, he'd have been home free. Instead, he looked over his shoulder to see if anyone was chasing him (three of us were)... and ran full speed into a glass bus shelter. Glass exploded everywhere, he hit the ground like wet cement and started bleeding from a decent head wound, and it was fantastic. Cartoon violence, instant karma, and a grateful lady who bought the three of us Starbucks for giving chase.

#5552. Three Green Hydrogen Projects Slip Through The Trump Chopper

When US President Donald Trump swept into office last year, he set his choppers upon the $8 billion Regional Clean Hydrogen Hubs program, snuffing out plans to establish a diversified, domestic hydrogen market with a focus on decarbonization. Nevertheless, some stakeholders have continued to pursue the dream of producing green hydrogen from renewable resources — instead of squeezing it from natural gas. Now all that hard work is beginning to pay off, Trump or no Trump.

If you guessed the startup Plug Power is in the mix, run right out and buy yourself a cigar. Plug Power began life back in 1997 with a focus on electric forklifts powered by zero emission hydrogen fuel cells. In addition to replacing fossil fuels, Plug Power aimed to replace battery-powered forklifts, with fuel cells being a better fit for the fast-paced environment of shipping and logistics.

Plug Power soon pivoted into replacing gas-derived hydrogen fuel with so-named green hydrogen, pushed from water by an electrical current. After a series of ups and downs the company hit the jackpot in January of 2025, when the US Department of Energy tapped it for a $1.66 loan guarantee in support of the Regional Clean Hydrogen Hubs program, which was funded through the 2021 Bipartisan Infrastructure Law. If all went according to plan, Plug Power would have used the loan to finance up to six green hydrogen projects around the US, all affiliated with the Hydrogen Hubs program.

So much for that. The Energy Department announced the loan award at the beginning of the month, when former President Joe Biden was still in office. However, by the end of the month Trump was officially installed in the White House. He soon pulled the plug on the Plug Power loan, so to speak, sending it down the drain alongside many other federally funded clean power projects.

That was not the end of the story for Plug Power. After all, it’s a big world out there, and Plug Power continued to pitch its wares overseas. Last week the company also relaunched itself back into the US market, bigly.

On November 17, Plug Power announced that its new “GenEco™ Scale” green hydrogen electrolyzer system is up for grabs in the US market while concurrently announcing that its first customer is the Texas-based firm Aracdia eFuels. Arcadia will deploy 280 megawatts’ worth of GenEco Scale modules at its Project ARC campus in the Portland/Corpus Christi area in Texas. When fully up and running, Project ARC will deploy green hydrogen from the GenEco Scale system to produce about 23.2 million gallons of fossil-free aviation fuel per year.

While green hydrogen has its fans in the decarbonization field, the stubbornly high cost of electrolysis has raised a formidable obstacle against widespread commercial uptake. Plug Power is among the stakeholders pitching modular, integrated systems to help bring costs down into parity with conventional hydrogen sourced from natural gas or coal, and that’s where the GenEco system comes into play.

“Built around a standardized 20 MW block architecture, GenEco Scale combines Plug’s proven proton exchange membrane (PEM) electrolyzer technology with integrated balance-of-plant systems into a modular, pre-engineered solution for hydrogen production projects of approximately 100 MW and above,” Plug Power explained in a press statement last week. The company also noted that the “outdoor-ready” system avoids much of the cost and time related to the buildings and other infrastructure required of traditional large scale electrolyzer systems.

Arcadia eFuels CEO Amy Hebert also chipped in her two cents in support of the modular system. “Reducing the engineering burden on our EPC [engineering, procurement, and construction] partners and minimizing the complexity of bringing these systems together were important considerations for Project ARC,” Herbert emphasized.

Savings on land is another part of the cost-cutting plan. Unlike conventional electrolyzer system layouts, the GenEco model deploys a vertical configuration. The vertical system requires less than one acre per 100 megawatts of electrolyzer capacity, according to Plug Power.

Another Plug Power project has bubbled up in California, which was on track to host a suite of projects under the Hydrogen Hubs program with a focus on hydrogen derived from biomass as well as water electrolysis. The funding evaporated under Trump, but the state is still getting its hands on green hydrogen.

On November 19, Plug Power announced that its containerized 5-megawatt GenEco electrolyzer is up and running at a green hydrogen plant in Vernon, California, under the umbrella of the New Jersey-based firm Avina Clean Hydrogen.

Green hydrogen from this project will go to fulfill California’s plans for a zero-emission freight corridor, the plan for which includes fuel cell electric trucks as well as battery-powered trucks. “Located approximately 10 miles from the Port of Long Beach, the facility is designed to serve fleets moving freight through the Los Angeles–Long Beach port complex. It combines on-site fueling with tube-trailer deliveries to customers across the Los Angeles region,” Plug Power explains.

The station, also accommodate buses, and it can refuel a heavy-duty vehicle to more than 95% capacity in less than 30 minutes, according to Plug Power. That stacks up well against charging times for battery-powered heavy duty vehicles. On its website, for example, Tesla asserts that its new Semi electric truck can recover 60% of its 500 mile range in up to 30 minutes at a Tesla Megacharger station.

For the record, Avina has not placed all of its cards on hydrogen fuel cell trucks. The company’s roster of clean hydrogen projects also includes SAF (sustainable aviation fuel) and green ammonia, which is a key input for fertilizer among other chemical products.

Rounding out the trio of new projects is the Swedish firm PowerCell Group. The firm is better known for its work in the marine energy field, and now it is tapping into land-based markets as well. Last week PowerCell announced that its US branch is in motion to set up a green hydrogen system run by excess electricity from an existing solar array owned by a to-be-named client in the US. In effect, the hydrogen will serve as an energy storage medium. It will go to run a PowerCell PowerPod 3000 stationary fuel cell, which will kick into action at night to keep the clean kilowatts flowing after the sun goes down.

PowerCell describes the PowerPod 3000 as “a containerized power generation platform integrating our PS190 systems with power conversion equipment, cooling and DMC, PowerCell’s control and Energy Orchestration technology,” with an installed fuel cell capacity of 27 megawatts and 20 megawatts of rated power.

That’s a good question. Rather than let that excess solar electricity go to waste, the client could invest in a BESS (battery energy storage system) for daytime energy storage and night-time electricity. However, a typical BESS only lasts for a handful of hours. In contrast, PowerCell describes its fuel cell as a “long duration” storage system, which generally means eight hours or more.

PowerCell also points out that a fuel cell does something that a BESS cannot. Fuel cells generate electricity by combining hydrogen with oxygen in the presence of a catalyst. Water — H2O — is a byproduct. All else being equal, that opens up the potential for green hydrogen to serve as a water distribution medium, sucking up water in an electrolysis system at one location, then pushing it out in a fuel cell located elsewhere.

PowerCell is not making any specific promises for this particular project, though the company does emphasize that “the ability to generate both electricity and recoverable water adds another dimension of value to hydrogen-based energy storage.”

“The amount of water recovered, and its potential uses will depend on the final system configuration, operating conditions and water treatment requirements,” the company adds.

And, that’s where things get interesting. The client is a to-be-named electric cooperative, which is of interest because electric cooperatives (more specifically, Rural Electric Cooperatives) account for a large portion of the US grid, and their roots in the rural poverty fighting programs of the Great Depression provide for a distinctive public benefit mission that can, in some cases, allow more wiggle room for innovative energy solutions that generate broad benefits for local communities.

I’m reaching out to PowerCell for more details. If they can’t divulge the name of the client yet, perhaps they’ll be able to describe what they mean by long duration energy storage, so stay tuned for an update.

Photo: The startup Plug Power is among the green hydrogen stakeholders carving out more space for renewable energy in the US market, despite last year’s sharp U-turn in federal energy policy (cropped, courtesy of Plug Power).

Tina has been covering advanced energy technology, military sustainability, emerging materials, biofuels, ESG and related policy and political matters for CleanTechnica since 2009. Follow her @tinamcasey on LinkedIn, Mastodon or Bluesky.
